\par\par
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{matrix}.

(105)

We now prove that, given the symmetry localization property for 3-anyon states in Eq. (
5
), we can generalize it to short-range correlated
n
-anyon states with finitely many anyons that are far away from each other. We will see that the following relation holds approximately:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &R_{g}|a^{(1)}_{1},a^{(2)}_{2},\cdots,\bar{a}^{(n)}_{n};{\{e\};\{\mu\}}\rangle\propto\sum_{\{\mu^{\prime}\}}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}\\
&\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\Big(V_{g}^{a^{(i)}}(^{g}x_{i})\Big)V_{g}^{a^{(n)}}(^{g}x_{n})|^{g}\hskip-1.0pta^{(1)}_{{}^{g}\hskip-1.0pt1},^{g}\hskip-1.0pta^{(2)}_{{}^{g}\hskip-1.0pt2},\cdots,^{g}\bar{a}^{(n)}_{{}^{g}n};\{^{g}\hskip-1.0pte\};\{\mu^{\prime}\}\rangle.\end{aligned}

(106)

In the above,

\displaystyle|a^{(1)}_{1},a^{(2)}_{2},\cdots,\bar{a}^{(n)}_{n};{\{e\};\{\mu\}}\rangle=|a^{(1)}_{1},a^{(2)}_{2},\cdots,\bar{a}^{(n)}_{n};e^{(1)},e^{(2)},\cdots,e^{(n-3)};\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\cdots,\mu_{n-2}\rangle

(107)

and

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}\\
=&[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)};^{g}e^{(1)})]_{\mu_{1}\mu_{1}^{\prime}}[U_{g}(^{g}e^{(1)},^{g}a^{(3)};^{g}e^{(2)})]_{\mu_{2}\mu_{2}^{\prime}}\cdots[U_{g}(^{g}e^{(n-3)},^{g}a^{(n-1)};^{g}a^{(n)})]_{\mu_{n-2}\mu_{n-2}^{\prime}}.\end{aligned}

(108)

As before, the
V
operators above are the same as in Eq. (
5
). This relation should be well expected, since it just means that the symmetry actions on a multi-anyon state have the three anticipated effects: changing the types and positions of the anyons, changing the fusion vertex basis states, and changing the local information. The nontrivial part of our analysis is to bound the error in this relation for states where the anyons are separated by finite distances. In the rest of this appendix, we derive this bound, which is given by Eq. (
124
).

For notational simplicity, denote the left and right hand sides of Eq. (
106
) by
|\psi_{1}^{(n)}\rangle
and
|\psi_{2}^{(n)}\rangle
, respectively. Also, denote the density matrix of any pure state
|\psi\rangle
by
\rho_{|\psi\rangle}
. We will relate
||\rho_{|\psi_{1}^{(n+1)}\rangle}-\rho_{|\psi_{2}^{(n+1)}\rangle}||_{1}
to
||\rho_{|\psi_{1}^{(n)}\rangle}-\rho_{|\psi_{2}^{(n)}\rangle}||_{1}
or its analog.
11
11

11

The trace norm of an operator
O
is
||O||_{1}={\rm Tr}(\sqrt{O^{\dagger}O})
.
Because
||\rho_{|\psi_{1}^{(3)}\rangle}-\rho_{|\psi_{2}^{(3)}\rangle}||_{1}=0
according to Eq. (
5
), such a relation allows us to bound
||\rho_{|\psi_{1}^{(n)}\rangle}-\rho_{|\psi_{2}^{(n)}\rangle}||_{1}
for any
n
.

To proceed, note that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\psi_{1}^{(n+1)}\rangle=&R_{g}|a^{(1)}_{1},a^{(2)}_{2},\cdots,a^{(n)}_{n},\bar{a}^{(n+1)}_{n+1};e^{(1)},e^{(2)},\cdots,e^{(n-2)};\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\cdots,\mu_{n-1}\rangle\\
=&R_{g}(S^{a^{(n)}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}_{\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu_{n-1}})_{n+1,n}R_{g}^{-1}R_{g}|a^{(1)}_{1},a^{(2)}_{2},\cdots,a^{(n-1)}_{n-1},\bar{e}^{(n-2)};e^{(1)},e^{(2)},\cdots,e^{(n-3)};\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\cdots,\mu_{n-2}\rangle\\
=&R_{g}(S^{a^{(n)}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}_{\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu_{n-1}})_{n+1,n}R_{g}^{-1}|\psi_{1}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle\end{aligned}

(109)

and

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &|\psi_{2}^{(n+1)}\rangle\\
=&\sum_{\{\mu^{\prime}\}}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n)},^{g}a^{(n+1)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Big(V_{g}^{a^{(i)}}(^{g}x_{i})\Big)V_{g}^{\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}(^{g}x_{n+1})\\
&|^{g}\hskip-1.0pta^{(1)}_{{}^{g}\hskip-1.0pt1},^{g}\hskip-1.0pta^{(2)}_{{}^{g}\hskip-1.0pt2},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n)}_{{}^{g}n},^{g}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}_{{}^{g}(n+1)};\{^{g}\hskip-1.0pte\},\{\mu^{\prime}\}\rangle\\
=&\sum_{\{\mu^{\prime}\}}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n)},^{g}a^{(n+1)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Big(V_{g}^{a^{(i)}}(^{g}x_{i})\Big)V_{g}^{\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}(^{g}x_{n+1})\\
&(S^{{}^{g}a^{(n)}{{}^{g}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}}_{{}^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu^{\prime}_{n-1}})_{{}^{g}(n+1)^{g}n}|^{g}a^{(1)}_{{}^{g}1},^{g}a^{(2)}_{{}^{g}2},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n-1)}_{{}^{g}(n-1)},^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}n};^{g}e^{(1)},^{g}e^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}e^{(n-3)};\mu_{1}^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime},\cdots,\mu_{n-2}^{\prime}\rangle,\end{aligned}

(110)

with

\displaystyle|\psi_{1}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle=R_{g}|a^{(1)}_{1},a^{(2)}_{2},\cdots,a^{(n-1)}_{n-1},\bar{e}^{(n-2)};e^{(1)},e^{(2)},\cdots,e^{(n-3)};\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\cdots,\mu_{n-2}\rangle.

(111)

Then

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &||\rho_{|\psi_{1}^{(n+1)}\rangle}-\rho_{|\psi_{2}^{(n+1)}\rangle}||_{1}\\
\leqslant&||\rho_{|\psi_{1}^{(n+1)}\rangle}-\rho_{R_{g}(S^{a^{(n)}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}_{\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu_{n-1}})_{n+1,n}R_{g}^{-1}|\psi_{2}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle}||_{1}+||\rho_{R_{g}(S^{a^{(n)}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}_{\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu_{n-1}})_{n+1,n}R_{g}^{-1}|\psi_{2}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle}-\rho_{|\psi_{2}^{(n+1)}\rangle}||_{1}\\
=&||\rho_{|\psi_{1}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle}-\rho_{|\psi_{2}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle}||_{1}+||\rho_{R_{g}(S^{a^{(n)}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}_{\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu_{n-1}})_{n+1,n}R_{g}^{-1}|\psi_{2}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle}-\rho_{|\psi_{2}^{(n+1)}\rangle}||_{1}\\
\leqslant&||\rho_{|\psi_{1}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle}-\rho_{|\psi_{2}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle}||_{1}+2\sqrt{1-F(R_{g}(S^{a^{(n)}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}_{\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu_{n-1}})_{n+1,n}R_{g}^{-1}|\psi_{2}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle,|\psi_{2}^{(n+1)}\rangle)},\\
\end{aligned}

(112)

where the first inequality is the triangle inequality, the last inequality is the Fuchs-van de Graaf inequality, with the fidelity between two pure states
|\alpha\rangle
and
|\beta\rangle
defined as
F(|\alpha\rangle,|\beta\rangle)=|\langle\alpha|\beta\rangle|^{2}
, and

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &|\psi_{2}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle\\
=&\sum_{\{\mu^{\prime}\}}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n-1)},^{g}e^{(n-2)};^{g}e^{(1)},^{g}e^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}e^{(n-3)})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}\\
&\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\Big(V_{g}^{a^{(i)}}(^{g}x_{i})\Big)V_{g}^{\bar{e}^{(n-2)}}(^{g}x_{n})|^{g}a^{(1)}_{{}^{g}1},^{g}a^{(2)}_{{}^{g}2},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n-1)}_{{}^{g}(n-1)},^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}n};^{g}e^{(1)},^{g}e^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}e^{(n-3)};\mu^{\prime}_{1},\mu^{\prime}_{2},\cdots,\mu^{\prime}_{n-2}\rangle.\end{aligned}

(113)

Next, we need the fidelity in the second term of Eq. (
112
), which can be obtained from

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\langle\psi_{2}^{(n+1)}|R_{g}(S^{a^{(n)}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}_{\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu_{n-1}})_{n+1,n}R_{g}^{-1}|\psi_{2}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle\\
=&\sum_{\{\mu^{\prime}\mu^{\prime\prime}\}}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n)},^{g}a^{(n+1)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}^{*}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n-1)},^{g}e^{(n-2)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime\prime}\}}\\
&\cdot\langle^{g}a^{(1)}_{{}^{g}1},^{g}a^{(2)}_{{}^{g}2},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n-1)}_{{}^{g}(n-1)},^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}n};^{g}e^{(1)},^{g}e^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}e^{(n-3)};\mu_{1}^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime},\cdots,\mu_{n-2}^{\prime}|(S^{{}^{g}a^{(n)}{{}^{g}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}}_{{}^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu^{\prime}_{n-1}})_{{}^{g}(n+1)^{g}n}^{\dagger}\\
&\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Big(V_{g}^{a^{(i)}}(^{g}x_{i})\Big)^{\dagger}\Big(V_{g}^{\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}(^{g}x_{n+1})\Big)^{\dagger}R_{g}(S^{a^{(n)}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}_{\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu_{n-1}})_{n+1,n}R_{g}^{-1}\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\Big(V_{g}^{a^{(j)}}(^{g}x_{j})\Big)V_{g}^{\bar{e}^{(n-2)}}(^{g}x_{n})\\
&\cdot|^{g}a^{(1)}_{{}^{g}1},^{g}a^{(2)}_{{}^{g}2},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n-1)}_{{}^{g}(n-1)},^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}n};^{g}e^{(1)},^{g}e^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}e^{(n-3)};\mu^{\prime\prime}_{1},\mu^{\prime\prime}_{2},\cdots,\mu^{\prime\prime}_{n-2}\rangle\\
=&\sum_{\{\mu^{\prime}\mu^{\prime\prime}\}}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n)},^{g}a^{(n+1)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}^{*}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n-1)},^{g}e^{(n-2)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime\prime}\}}\\
&\cdot\langle^{g}a^{(1)}_{{}^{g}1},^{g}a^{(2)}_{{}^{g}2},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n-1)}_{{}^{g}(n-1)},^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}n};^{g}e^{(1)},^{g}e^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}e^{(n-3)};\mu_{1}^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime},\cdots,\mu_{n-2}^{\prime}|(S^{{}^{g}a^{(n)}{{}^{g}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}}_{{}^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu^{\prime}_{n-1}})_{{}^{g}(n+1)^{g}n}^{\dagger}\\
&\cdot\Big(V_{g}^{a^{(n)}}(^{g}x_{n})\Big)^{\dagger}\Big(V_{g}^{\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}(^{g}x_{n+1})\Big)^{\dagger}R_{g}(S^{a^{(n)}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}_{\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu_{n-1}})_{n+1,n}R_{g}^{-1}V_{g}^{\bar{e}^{(n-2)}}(^{g}x_{n})\\
&\cdot|^{g}a^{(1)}_{{}^{g}1},^{g}a^{(2)}_{{}^{g}2},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n-1)}_{{}^{g}(n-1)},^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}n};^{g}e^{(1)},^{g}e^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}e^{(n-3)};\mu^{\prime\prime}_{1},\mu^{\prime\prime}_{2},\cdots,\mu^{\prime\prime}_{n-2}\rangle\\
=&\sum_{\{\mu^{\prime}\}}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n)},^{g}a^{(n+1)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}^{*}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n-1)},^{g}e^{(n-2)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}\\
&\cdot\langle^{g}e_{{}^{g}1}^{(n-2)},^{g}\bar{e}_{{}^{g}n}^{(n-2)}|O^{\dagger}\\
&\cdot(S^{{}^{g}a^{(n)}{{}^{g}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}}_{{}^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu^{\prime}_{n-1}})_{{}^{g}(n+1)^{g}n}^{\dagger}\Big(V_{g}^{a^{(n)}}(^{g}x_{n})\Big)^{\dagger}\Big(V_{g}^{\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}(^{g}x_{n+1})\Big)^{\dagger}R_{g}(S^{a^{(n)}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}_{\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu_{n-1}})_{n+1,n}R_{g}^{-1}V_{g}^{\bar{e}^{(n-2)}}(^{g}x_{n})\\
&\cdot O|^{g}e_{{}^{g}1}^{(n-2)},^{g}\bar{e}_{{}^{g}n}^{(n-2)}\rangle,\end{aligned}

(114)

where
O
is a product of some splitting and moving operators supported around
{}^{g}x_{1},^{g}x_{2},\cdots,^{g}x_{n-1}
, such that

\displaystyle O|^{g}e_{{}^{g}1}^{(n-2)},^{g}\bar{e}_{{}^{g}n}^{(n-2)}\rangle=|^{g}a^{(1)}_{{}^{g}1},^{g}a^{(2)}_{{}^{g}2},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n-1)}_{{}^{g}(n-1)},^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}n};^{g}e^{(1)},^{g}e^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}e^{(n-3)};\mu^{\prime\prime}_{1},\mu^{\prime\prime}_{2},\cdots,\mu^{\prime\prime}_{n-2}\rangle.

(115)

According to Ref.
Wolf et al. 2008
, the above equation implies that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|&\langle\psi_{2}^{(n+1)}|R_{g}(S^{a^{(n)}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}_{\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu_{n-1}})_{n+1,n}R_{g}^{-1}|\psi_{2}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle\\
&-\sum_{\{\mu^{\prime}\}}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n)},^{g}a^{(n+1)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}^{*}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n-1)},^{g}e^{(n-2)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}\\
&\cdot\langle^{g}e_{{}^{g}1}^{(n-2)},^{g}\bar{e}_{{}^{g}n}^{(n-2)}|O^{\dagger}O|^{g}e_{{}^{g}1}^{(n-2)},^{g}\bar{e}_{{}^{g}n}^{(n-2)}\rangle\\
&\cdot\langle^{g}e_{{}^{g}1}^{(n-2)},^{g}\bar{e}_{{}^{g}n}^{(n-2)}|(S^{{}^{g}a^{(n)}{{}^{g}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}}_{{}^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu^{\prime}_{n-1}})_{{}^{g}(n+1)^{g}n}^{\dagger}\Big(V_{g}^{a^{(n)}}(^{g}x_{n})\Big)^{\dagger}\Big(V_{g}^{\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}(^{g}x_{n+1})\Big)^{\dagger}\\
&R_{g}(S^{a^{(n)}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}_{\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu_{n-1}})_{n+1,n}R_{g}^{-1}V_{g}^{\bar{e}^{(n-2)}}(^{g}x_{n})|^{g}e_{{}^{g}1}^{(n-2)},^{g}\bar{e}_{{}^{g}n}^{(n-2)}\rangle\Bigg|\\
\leqslant&\sqrt{2I_{|^{g}e^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}1},^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}n}\rangle}(A_{{}^{g}1,^{g}2,\cdots,^{g}(n-1)}:A_{{}^{g}n,^{g}(n+1)})}\\
&\Bigg|\sum_{\{\mu^{\prime}\}}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n)},^{g}a^{(n+1)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}^{*}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n-1)},^{g}e^{(n-2)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}\Bigg|.\end{aligned}

(116)

Here
A_{{}^{g}1,^{g}2,\cdots,^{g}(n-1)}
and
A_{{}^{g}n,^{g}(n+1)}
are the smallest rectangular regions that cover
{}^{g}x_{1},^{g}x_{2},\cdots,^{g}x_{n-1}
and
{}^{g}x_{n},^{g}x_{n+1}
, respectively.

Using Eq. (
26
), the left hand side of Eq. (
116
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|&\langle\psi_{2}^{(n+1)}|R_{g}(S^{a^{(n)}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}_{\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu_{n-1}})_{n+1,n}R_{g}^{-1}|\psi_{2}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle\\
&-\sum_{\{\mu^{\prime}\}}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n)},^{g}a^{(n+1)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}^{*}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n-1)},^{g}e^{(n-2)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}\\
&\cdot[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(n)},^{g}\bar{a}^{(n+1)};^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)})]_{\mu_{n-1}\mu^{\prime}_{n-1}}\Bigg|\end{aligned}

(117)

The second term in the above can be simplified using a cyclic property,
U_{g}(a,b;c)\propto U_{g}(b,\bar{c};\bar{a})
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\sum_{\{\mu^{\prime}\}}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n)},^{g}a^{(n+1)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}^{*}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n-1)},^{g}e^{(n-2)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}\\
&\cdot[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(n)},^{g}\bar{a}^{(n+1)};^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)})]_{\mu_{n-1}\mu^{\prime}_{n-1}}\\
\propto&\sum_{\mu^{\prime}_{1},\mu^{\prime}_{2},\cdots,\mu^{\prime}_{n-1}}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)};^{g}e^{(1)})]^{*}_{\mu_{1}\mu^{\prime}_{1}}[U_{g}(^{g}e^{(1)},^{g}a^{(3)};^{g}e^{(2)})]^{*}_{\mu_{2}\mu^{\prime}_{2}}\cdots[U_{g}(^{g}e^{(n-2)},^{g}a^{(n)};^{g}a^{(n+1)})]^{*}_{\mu_{n-1}\mu^{\prime}_{n-1}}\\
&\cdot[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)};^{g}e^{(1)})]_{\mu_{1}\mu^{\prime}_{1}}[U_{g}(^{g}e^{(1)},^{g}a^{(3)};^{g}e^{(2)})]_{\mu_{2}\mu^{\prime}_{2}}\cdots[U_{g}(^{g}e^{(n-3)},^{g}a^{(n-1)};^{g}e^{(n-2)})]_{\mu_{n-2}\mu^{\prime}_{n-2}}\\
&\cdot[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(n)},^{g}\bar{a}^{(n+1)};^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)})]_{\mu_{n-1}\mu^{\prime}_{n-1}}\\
=&1.\end{aligned}

(118)

The cyclic property is a consequence of Eqs. (
5
) and (
26
):

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &[U_{g}(a,b;c)]_{\mu\nu}\\
\propto&\langle a_{1},b_{2},\bar{c}_{0^{\prime}};\nu|V^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptb}_{g}(x_{2})^{\dagger}V^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{g}(x_{1})^{\dagger}V^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{c}}_{g}(x_{0^{\prime}})^{\dagger}\cdot R_{g}|^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt1},^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptb_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt2},^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{c}_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt0^{\prime}};\mu\rangle\\
\propto&\langle a_{1},\bar{a}_{2}|(S^{b\bar{c}}_{\bar{a},\nu})_{0^{\prime}2}^{\dagger}V^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptb}_{g}(x_{2})^{\dagger}V^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{g}(x_{1})^{\dagger}V^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{c}}_{g}(x_{0^{\prime}})^{\dagger}\cdot R_{g}(S^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptb^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{c}}_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{a},\nu})_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt{0^{\prime}}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt2}R^{-1}_{g}V^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{g}(x_{1})V^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{a}}_{g}(x_{2})|a_{1},\bar{a}_{2}\rangle\\
=&U(b,\bar{c};\bar{a})_{\mu\nu}.\end{aligned}

(119)

The right hand side of Eq. (
116
) can be bounded using the triangle inequality and Cauchy-Schwartz inequality:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\sqrt{2I_{|^{g}e^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}1},^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}n}\rangle}(A_{{}^{g}1,^{g}2,\cdots,^{g}(n-1)}:A_{{}^{g}n,^{g}(n+1)})}\\
&\Bigg|\sum_{\{\mu^{\prime}\}}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n)},^{g}a^{(n+1)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}^{*}[U_{g}(^{g}a^{(1)},^{g}a^{(2)},\cdots,^{g}a^{(n-1)},^{g}e^{(n-2)};\{^{g}e\})]_{\{\mu\}\{\mu^{\prime}\}}\Bigg|\\
=&\sqrt{2I_{|^{g}e^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}1},^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}n}\rangle}(A_{{}^{g}1,^{g}2,\cdots,^{g}(n-1)}:A_{{}^{g}n,^{g}(n+1)})}\sum_{\mu^{\prime}_{n-1}}[U_{g}(^{g}e^{(n-2)},^{g}a^{(n)};^{g}a^{(n+1)})]^{*}_{\mu_{n-1}\mu^{\prime}_{n-1}}\\
\leqslant&\sqrt{2N_{e^{(n-2)}a^{(n)}}^{a^{(n+1)}}I_{|^{g}e^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}1},^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}n}\rangle}(A_{{}^{g}1,^{g}2,\cdots,^{g}(n-1)}:A_{{}^{g}n,^{g}(n+1)})}.\end{aligned}

(120)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\sqrt{F(R_{g}(S^{a^{(n)}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}_{\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu_{n-1}})_{n+1,n}R_{g}^{-1}|\psi_{2}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle,|\psi_{2}^{(n+1)}\rangle)}\\
\geqslant&1-\sqrt{2N_{e^{(n-2)}a^{(n)}}^{a^{(n+1)}}I_{|^{g}e^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}1},^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}n}\rangle}(A_{{}^{g}1,^{g}2,\cdots,^{g}(n-1)}:A_{{}^{g}n,^{g}(n+1)})}.\end{aligned}

(121)

Or, equivalently,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &F(R_{g}(S^{a^{(n)}\bar{a}^{(n+1)}}_{\bar{e}^{(n-2)},\mu_{n-1}})_{n+1,n}R_{g}^{-1}|\psi_{2}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle,|\psi_{2}^{(n+1)}\rangle)\\
\geqslant&\Bigg[{\rm max}\Bigg\{1-\sqrt{2N_{e^{(n-2)}a^{(n)}}^{a^{(n+1)}}I_{|^{g}e^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}1},^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}n}\rangle}(A_{{}^{g}1,^{g}2,\cdots,^{g}(n-1)}:A_{{}^{g}n,^{g}(n+1)})},0\Bigg\}\Bigg]^{2}\end{aligned}

(122)

Then Eq. (
112
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &||\rho_{|\psi_{1}^{(n+1)}\rangle}-\rho_{|\psi_{2}^{(n+1)}\rangle}||_{1}\\
\leqslant&||\rho_{|\psi_{1}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle}-\rho_{|\psi_{2}^{(n)^{\prime}}\rangle}||_{1}\\
&+2\Bigg\{1-\Bigg[{\rm max}\Bigg\{1-\sqrt{2N_{e^{(n-2)}a^{(n)}}^{a^{(n+1)}}I_{|^{g}e^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}1},^{g}\bar{e}^{(n-2)}_{{}^{g}n}\rangle}(A_{{}^{g}1,^{g}2,\cdots,^{g}(n-1)}:A_{{}^{g}n,^{g}(n+1)})},0\Bigg\}\Bigg]^{2}\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{aligned}

(123)

Iterating the above relation, we get

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &||\rho_{|\psi_{1}^{(n)}\rangle}-\rho_{|\psi_{2}^{(n)}\rangle}||_{1}\\
\leqslant&2\sum_{i=4}^{n}\Bigg\{1-\Bigg[{\rm max}\Bigg\{1-\sqrt{2N_{e^{(i-3)}a^{(i-1)}}^{a^{(i)}}I_{|^{g}e^{(i-3)}_{{}^{g}1},^{g}\bar{e}^{(i-3)}_{{}^{g}(i-1)}\rangle}(A_{{}^{g}1,^{g}2,\cdots,^{g}(i-2)}:A_{{}^{g}(i-1),^{g}(i)})},0\Bigg\}\Bigg]^{2}\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{aligned}

(124)

If the anyon states under consideration are short-range correlated and all anyons are far apart, the above bound approaches zero exponentially as the distances between the anyons increase. It is interesting to note the dependence on the fusion multiplicity in this bound, which suggests that the error becomes larger when the dimension of the space of fusion vertex basis states becomes larger.

Appendix D
Approximate opereator factorization

In Sec.
III
, we have claimed that Eq. (
13
) can always be achieved approximately by choosing the phases of the
V
operators. Similar treatments are also used multiple times later. In this appendix, we explain why this is valid and discuss the error in this approximation.

We start by writing
R_{g}|^{\bar{g}}a_{{}^{\bar{g}}1},^{\bar{g}}\bar{a}_{{}^{\bar{g}}0}\rangle
in two different ways:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} R_{g}|^{\bar{g}}a_{{}^{\bar{g}}1},^{\bar{g}}\bar{a}_{{}^{\bar{g}}0}\rangle&\propto V_{g}^{{}^{\bar{g}}a}(x_{1})V_{g}^{{}^{\bar{g}}\bar{a}}(x_{0})|a_{1},\bar{a}_{0}\rangle\\
&\propto V_{g}^{{}^{\bar{g}}a}(x_{1})V_{g}^{{}^{\bar{g}}\bar{a}}(x_{0})M^{a}_{12}|a_{2},\bar{a}_{0}\rangle\\
&=V_{g}^{{}^{\bar{g}}a}(x_{1})M^{a}_{12}V_{g}^{{}^{\bar{g}}\bar{a}}(x_{0})|a_{2},\bar{a}_{0}\rangle,\\
R_{g}|^{\bar{g}}a_{{}^{\bar{g}}1},^{\bar{g}}\bar{a}_{{}^{\bar{g}}0}\rangle&\propto R_{g}M^{{}^{\bar{g}}a}_{{}^{\bar{g}}1^{\bar{g}}2}|^{\bar{g}}a_{{}^{\bar{g}}2},^{\bar{g}}\bar{a}_{{}^{\bar{g}}0}\rangle=R_{g}M^{{}^{\bar{g}}a}_{{}^{\bar{g}}1^{\bar{g}}2}R_{g}^{-1}R_{g}|^{\bar{g}}a_{{}^{\bar{g}}2},^{\bar{g}}\bar{a}_{{}^{\bar{g}}0}\rangle\\
&\propto R_{g}M^{{}^{\bar{g}}a}_{{}^{\bar{g}}1^{\bar{g}}2}R_{g}^{-1}V_{g}^{{}^{\bar{g}}a}(x_{2})V_{g}^{{}^{\bar{g}}\bar{a}}(x_{0})|a_{2},\bar{a}_{0}\rangle,\end{aligned}

(125)

We see that
V_{g}^{{}^{\bar{g}}a}(x_{1})M^{a}_{12}
and
R_{g}M^{{}^{\bar{g}}a}_{{}^{\bar{g}}1^{\bar{g}}2}R_{g}^{-1}V_{g}^{{}^{\bar{g}}a}(x_{2})
are unitary operators supported around
x_{1,2}
, and
V_{g}^{{}^{\bar{g}}\bar{a}}(x_{0})
is a unitary operator supported around
x_{0}
that is far away from
x_{1,2}
. The following theorem implies that the two sides of Eq. (
13
) only differ by a phase factor approximately, with an error exponentially small in the distance between
x_{0}
and
x_{1,2}
.

Theorem D.1
.

Let
|\psi\rangle
be a normalized state,
P_{A}
and
Q_{A}
be two bounded operators supported in region
A
of Fig.
6
, and
P_{C}
and
Q_{C}
be two bounded operators supported in region
C
of Fig.
6
. Then

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &{\rm min}\left\{\sqrt{F(P_{A}|\psi\rangle,Q_{A}|\psi\rangle)},\sqrt{F(P_{C}|\psi\rangle,Q_{C}|\psi\rangle)}\right\}\\
&\geqslant\sqrt{F(P_{A}P_{C}|\psi\rangle,Q_{A}Q_{C}|\psi\rangle)}-\sqrt{2I_{|\psi\rangle}(A:C)}\cdot||P_{A}Q_{A}^{\dagger}||\cdot||P_{C}Q_{C}^{\dagger}||,\end{aligned}

(126)

where
F(|\psi_{1}\rangle,|\psi_{2}\rangle)=|\langle\psi_{1}|\psi_{2}\rangle|^{2}
is the fidelity between the states
|\psi_{1}\rangle
and
|\psi_{2}\rangle
, and
I_{|\psi\rangle}(A:C)
is the mutual information between regions
A
and
C
of the state
|\psi\rangle
in Fig.
6
.

Proof.

According to Ref.
Wolf et al. 2008
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &|\langle\psi|P_{A}Q_{A}^{\dagger}P_{C}Q_{C}^{\dagger}|\psi\rangle-\langle\psi|P_{A}Q_{A}^{\dagger}|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|P_{C}Q_{C}^{\dagger}|\psi\rangle|\leqslant\sqrt{2I_{|\psi\rangle}(A:C)}\cdot||P_{A}Q_{A}^{\dagger}||\cdot||P_{C}Q_{C}^{\dagger}||,\end{aligned}

(127)

so

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &|\sqrt{F(P_{A}P_{C}|\psi\rangle,Q_{A}Q_{C}|\psi\rangle)}-\sqrt{F(P_{A}|\psi\rangle,Q_{A}|\psi\rangle)F(P_{C}|\psi\rangle,Q_{C}|\psi\rangle)}|\\
=&|\langle\psi|P_{A}Q_{A}^{\dagger}P_{C}Q_{C}^{\dagger}|\psi\rangle-\langle\psi|P_{A}Q_{A}^{\dagger}|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|P_{C}Q_{C}^{\dagger}|\psi\rangle|\\
\leqslant&\sqrt{2I_{|\psi\rangle}(A:C)}\cdot||P_{A}Q_{A}^{\dagger}||\cdot||P_{C}Q_{C}^{\dagger}||,\end{aligned}

(128)

Using that
0\leqslant F(P_{A,C}|\psi\rangle,Q_{A,C}|\psi\rangle)\leqslant 1
, we get Eq. (
126
).
∎

As an application of Theorem
D.1
, for a short-range correlated state
|\psi\rangle
, if
P_{A}P_{C}|\psi\rangle
and
Q_{A}Q_{C}|\psi\rangle
only differ by a phase and if
A
and
C
are far away from each other so that
\sqrt{2I_{|\psi\rangle}(A:C)}\cdot||P_{A}Q_{A}^{\dagger}||\cdot||P_{C}Q_{C}^{\dagger}||\leqslant\sqrt{F(P_{A}P_{C}|\psi\rangle,Q_{A}Q_{C}|\psi\rangle)}=1
, then

\displaystyle F(P_{A}|\psi\rangle,Q_{A}|\psi\rangle)\geqslant\left(1-\sqrt{2I_{|\psi\rangle}(A:C)}\cdot||P_{A}Q_{A}^{\dagger}||\cdot||P_{C}Q_{C}^{\dagger}||\right)^{2}.

(129)

Namely,
P_{A}|\psi\rangle
and
Q_{A}|\psi\rangle
are almost the same, up to an error that is exponentially small in the distance between
A
and
C
. The same applies to
P_{C}|\psi\rangle
and
Q_{C}|\psi\rangle
. Namely, the equivalence between the effect of the operator
P_{A}P_{C}
on
|\psi\rangle
and the effect of the operator
Q_{A}Q_{C}
on
|\psi\rangle
can be approximately factorized into the two regions
A
and
C
. We will often use this factorization in this work, while ignoring the exponentially small errors when the regions
A
and
C
are far away from each other.

We emphasize that the condition of short-range correlation is important for this factorization. An example where the factorization fails can be seen from the following 2-qubit example. Suppose
|\psi\rangle=(|0_{A}1_{C}\rangle+|1_{A}0_{C}\rangle)/\sqrt{2}
. One can verify that
P_{A}P_{C}|\psi\rangle=Q_{A}Q_{C}|\psi\rangle
, where
P_{A}=|0_{A}\rangle\langle 1_{A}|+|1_{A}\rangle\langle 0_{A}|
,
P_{C}=|0_{C}\rangle\langle 1_{C}|+|1_{C}\rangle\langle 0_{C}|
, and
Q_{A}=Q_{C}
are the identity operator. In this case, the fidelities
F(P_{A}|\psi\rangle,Q_{A}|\psi\rangle)=F(P_{C}|\psi\rangle,Q_{C}|\psi\rangle)=0
. Namely,
P_{A}|\psi\rangle
and
Q_{A}|\psi\rangle
are very different, and the reason is that the two qubits are strongly correlated.

Returning to Eq. (
125
), by taking
P_{A}=V_{g}^{{}^{\bar{g}}a}(x_{1})M^{a}_{12}
,
Q_{A}=R_{g}M^{{}^{\bar{g}}a}_{{}^{\bar{g}}1^{\bar{g}}2}R_{g}^{-1}V_{g}^{{}^{\bar{g}}a}(x_{2})
,
P_{C}=Q_{C}=V_{g}^{{}^{\bar{g}}\bar{a}}(x_{0})
and
|\psi\rangle=|^{\bar{g}}a_{{}^{\bar{g}}1},^{\bar{g}}\bar{a}_{{}^{\bar{g}}0}\rangle
, we conclude that the two sides of Eq. (
13
) only approximately differ by a phase factor, so by choosing the phases of the
V
operators appropriately, Eq. (
13
) can be achieved, with an error exponentially small in the distance between
x_{0}
and
x_{1,2}
, which will be ignored because these regions are far away from each other.

Appendix E
More on trivially universal properties

In Sec.
IV.2.1
, we have mentioned that if
O
is a generic microscopic operator that is unrelated to the moving and splitting operators (e.g.,
O
may be a Pauli operator on a qubit),
O_{W}=WOW^{\dagger}
, and
|\psi_{W}\rangle=W|\psi\rangle
with
|\psi\rangle
a ground state of a system in the topological phase, then although
\langle\psi_{W}|O_{W}|\psi_{W}\rangle
is invariant under the quasi-adiabatic continuation in Eq. (
6
), no nontrivial universal properties can be extracted from it. In this appendix, we elaborate on this point. As a concrete example, we can assume that
O=Z_{1}
, the Pauli-
Z
operator acting on the first qubit of the system.

First of all, because
O
is assumed to be unrelated to the moving and splitting operators, i.e., it is not a sum of products of some moving and splitting operators, we should not expect that
\langle\psi_{W}|O_{W}|\psi_{W}\rangle
has any meaningful relation with anyons. This observation already suggests that the fact that
\langle\psi_{W}|O_{W}|\psi_{W}\rangle
is invariant under the quasi-adiabatic continuation in Eq. (
6
) cannot help us characterize the underlying topological phase.

Next, let us examine which universal properties we can possibly extract from the fact that
\langle\psi_{W}|O_{W}|\psi_{W}\rangle
is invariant under the quasi-adiabatic continuation in Eq. (
6
). One attempt is to consider the statement that there exists a quasi-local unitary operator whose ground state expectation value in all systems of the topological phase takes a particular value. Here the quasi-local operator means an operator concentrated around a local region but can have some exponentially decaying tail. This statement is true, with the quasi-local operator being
O_{W}
and the particular value of the ground state expectation value being
\langle\psi|O|\psi\rangle
. However, this statement trivially holds for all quantum states, no matter whether the state is a ground state of the topological phase of interest. More precisely, given any complex number
z
with
|z|\leqslant 1
and any state
|\phi\rangle
, it is straightforward to find a unitary operator
O_{1}
acting only on the first qubit, such that the
\langle\phi|O_{1}|\phi\rangle=z
. Therefore, this universal statement is trivial and cannot help us characterize the underlying topological phase.

We should contrast the above trivial universal statement and the nontrivial universal properties we identify in the main text of the paper. The universal data discussed in the main text also takes the form of
\langle\psi_{W}|O_{W}|\psi_{W}\rangle
, but the operator
O
there are not some arbitrary microscopic operator. Instead, there
O
is made of some moving and splitting operators, as well as some symmetry action operators. These operators are very special and related to anyons, which is why they can imprint universal properties tied to the anyons. Moreover, quantities obtained from these operators satisfy some highly nontrivial relations, such as the pentagon and hexagon equations in Eqs. (
17
) and (
20
), which are specific properties of topological phases not shared by generic phases.

Appendix F
Review of Kawagoe-Levin’s
F
- and
R
-symbols

In this appendix, we review the microscopic definitions of
F
- and
R
- matrices by Kawagoe and Levin, which can be viewed as the
F
- and
R
-symbols in a braided tensor category
Kawagoe and Levin 2020
.

We fix
6
positions
x_{0}
,
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
,
x_{3}
,
x_{4}
, and
x_{0^{\prime}}
to host anyons (see Fig.
7
). To define the
F
-symbols, we start with a state
|d_{1},\bar{d}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle
and apply some moving and splitting operators to it, which leads to the two states below and the intermediate steps encode the processes in Fig.
7
(b):

\displaystyle|e,\mu,\nu\rangle=M^{b}_{12}M^{a}_{01}(S^{ab}_{e,\mu})_{21}M^{c}_{32}(S^{ec}_{d,\nu})_{21}|d_{1},\overline{d}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle,

(130)

\displaystyle|f,\rho,\sigma\rangle=M^{c}_{32}(S^{bc}_{f,\rho})_{21}M^{f}_{12}M^{a}_{01}(S^{af}_{d,\sigma})_{21}|d_{1},\overline{d}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle.

The Kawagoe-Levin
F
-matrix is defined as

\displaystyle[F^{abc}_{d}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}=\langle f,\rho,\sigma|e,\mu,\nu\rangle.

(131)

It is shown in Ref.
Kawagoe and Levin 2020
that, when changing the subjective choices in Eq. (
130
), the
F
-matrices Eq. (
131
) also transform in the same form as in Eq. (
24
). We repeat the transformation here.

[F^{abc}_{d}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\})}\to\sum_{\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}[F^{abc}_{d}]_{\{e,\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}\},\{f,\rho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}\}}\times[\Omega^{ab}_{e}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}[\Omega^{ec}_{d}]_{\nu\nu^{\prime}}[(\Omega^{bc}_{f})^{-1}]_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}[(\Omega^{af}_{d})^{-1}]_{\rho^{\prime}\rho}.

(132)

Moreover, in appendix A of Ref.
Kawagoe and Levin 2020
, it is proved that the
F
-matrices Eq. (
131
) satisfy the pentagon equation
Kawagoe and Levin 2020
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\delta}[F^{fcd}_{e}]_{(g,\beta,\gamma),(l,\nu,\delta)}[F^{abl}_{e}]_{(f,\alpha,\delta),(k,\mu,\lambda)}=\sum_{h,\sigma,\psi,\rho}[F^{abc}_{g}]_{(f,\alpha,\beta),(h,\psi,\sigma)}[F^{ahd}_{e}]_{(g,\sigma,\gamma),(k,\rho,\lambda)}[F^{bcd}_{k}]_{(h,\psi,\rho),(l,\nu,\mu)}.

(133)

x_{0,1,2,3,4}
and

are far from each other, with

) and (

), respectively.

To define
R
-matrices, we start with a state
|c_{1},\bar{c}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle
and apply some moving and splitting operators to it, which leads to the following two states with the intermediate steps shown in Fig.
7
(c):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|\mu\rangle=M^{a}_{01}M^{a}_{12}M^{b}_{41}(S^{ba}_{c,\mu})_{21}|c_{1},\bar{c}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle,\\
&|\nu\rangle=M^{b}_{41}M^{b}_{12}M^{a}_{01}(S^{ab}_{c,\nu})_{21}|c_{1},\bar{c}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle.\end{split}

(134)

That is, the anyon
c
splits into
a
and
b
in two different ways, and, relative to the processes in
|\nu\rangle
, in the processes in
|\mu\rangle
the anyons
a
and
b
exchange their positions in a counter-clockwise manner. The Kawagoe-Levin
R
-matrix is defined as

\displaystyle[R^{ab}_{c}]_{\mu\nu}=\langle\nu|\mu\rangle.

(135)

There are two points to be clarified here, which were not discussed in Ref.
Kawagoe and Levin 2020
.

First, to let the
R
-matrices Eq. (
135
) be compatible with the convention in category theory, that is,
R^{ab}_{c}
results from the process where anyon
b
winds around anyon
a
for a half loop in a counter-clockwise direction, there are some constraints on the support of the moving operators. For each moving operator, there should exist a simply-connected region that 1) contains the support of this operator (after truncating its exponentially decaying tails discussed in Appendix
C.1
), 2) does not contain anyon positions that are not connected by this operator, and 3) does not intersect with the supports of the other moving operators. As such, the moving operators
M_{01}^{a_{1}}
,
M_{12}^{a_{2}}
, and
M_{41}^{a_{3}}
intersect only around
x_{1}
, for any anyons
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
and
a_{3}
in this TQSL. We then require that the supports of
M^{a}_{01}
,
M^{b}_{41}
and
M^{a}_{12}
(
M^{b}_{12}
) sweep around
x_{1}
in a counter-clockwise manner, as shown in Fig.
8
(a). In particular,
M^{a}_{12}
and
M^{b}_{12}
do not need to have the same support, but they should both satisfy the above requirement. In this way, it is guaranteed that the anyon
b
winds around the anyon
a
in a counter-clockwise direction during the process that is used to extract the
R
-matrix. In contrast, Fig.
8
(b) shows an example where the supports of
M^{a}_{01}
,
M^{b}_{51}
and
M^{a}_{12}
(
M^{b}_{12}
) sweep around
x_{1}
in a clockwise manner.

x_{0}
x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{4}
x_{0}
x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{5}
(a)
(b)
x_{0}
x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{5}
(c)
x_{4}

Figure 8:
(a) The supports of
M^{a}_{01}
,
M^{b}_{41}
and
M^{a}_{12}
(
M^{b}_{12}
) sweep around
x_{1}
in a counter-clockwise manner. (b) The supports of
M^{a}_{01}
,
M^{b}_{51}
and
M^{a}_{12}
(
M^{b}_{12}
) sweep around
x_{1}
in a clockwise manner. (c) We compare the
R
-matrices extracted with counter-clockwise sweeping moving operators and the
\hat{R}
-matrices extracted with clockwise sweeping moving operators.

Second, if the supports of
M^{a}_{01}
,
M^{b}_{41}
and
M^{a}_{12}
(
M^{b}_{12}
) sweep around
x_{1}
in a clockwise manner, then Eq. (
135
) gives
(R^{ba}_{c})^{-1}
instead of
R^{ab}_{c}
. To prove this, we still let the supports of
M^{a}_{01}
,
M^{b}_{41}
and
M^{a}_{12}
(
M^{b}_{12}
) sweep around
x_{1}
in a clockwise manner, and introduce an
x_{5}
with the supports of
M^{a}_{01}
,
M^{b}_{51}
and
M^{a}_{12}
(
M^{b}_{12}
) sweep around
x_{1}
in a clockwise manner (see Fig.
8
(c)). Consider the quantity extracted using
M^{a}_{01}
,
M^{b}_{51}
,
M^{a}_{12}
, and
M^{b}_{12}
via definition Eq. (
135
). We denote this quantity as
[\hat{R}^{ab}_{c}]_{\mu\nu}=_{5}\hskip-3.0pt\langle\nu|\mu\rangle_{5}
, where

\displaystyle|\mu\rangle_{5}=M^{a}_{01}M^{a}_{12}M^{b}_{51}(S^{ba}_{c,\mu})_{21}|c_{1},\bar{c}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle,

(136)

\displaystyle|\nu\rangle_{5}=M^{b}_{51}M^{b}_{12}M^{a}_{01}(S^{ab}_{c,\nu})_{21}|c_{1},\bar{c}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle.

To find the relation between these
\hat{R}
-matrices and the
R
-matrices in Eq. (
135
), we find two moving operators
M^{b}_{05}
and
M^{a}_{40}
that support in the dashed line in Fig.
8
(c). These two moving operators are taken to satisfy Eq. (
11
). Then we find that

\displaystyle M^{b}_{05}M^{a}_{40}|\mu\rangle_{5}

\displaystyle=M^{b}_{05}M^{a}_{40}M^{a}_{01}M^{a}_{12}M^{b}_{51}(S^{ba}_{c,\mu})_{21}|c_{1},\bar{c}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle

\displaystyle=M^{a}_{41}M^{a}_{12}M^{b}_{01}(S^{ba}_{c,\mu})_{21}|c_{1},\bar{c}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle\equiv|\mu^{\prime}\rangle,

\displaystyle M^{b}_{05}M^{a}_{40}|\nu\rangle_{5}

\displaystyle=M^{b}_{05}M^{a}_{40}M^{b}_{51}M^{b}_{12}M^{a}_{01}(S^{ab}_{c,\nu})_{21}|c_{1},\bar{c}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle

\displaystyle=M^{b}_{01}M^{b}_{12}M^{a}_{41}(S^{ab}_{c,\nu})_{21}|c_{1},\bar{c}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle\equiv|\nu^{\prime}\rangle.

We have used the fact that
M^{b}_{05}
commutes with
M^{a}_{41}
, and
M^{a}_{40}
commutes with
M^{b}_{51}
and
M^{b}_{12}
. On one hand, since the moving operators are chosen to be unitary, we have
{}_{5}\langle\nu|(M^{a}_{40})^{\dagger}(M^{b}_{05})^{\dagger}M^{b}_{05}M^{a}_{40}|\mu\rangle_{5}={{}_{5}}\langle\nu|\mu\rangle_{5}=[\hat{R}^{ab}_{c}]_{\mu\nu}
. On the other hand, we also notice that
\langle\nu^{\prime}|\mu^{\prime}\rangle=[R^{ba}_{c}]_{\nu\mu}^{*}
according to Eq. (
135
). Hence, we have
[\hat{R}^{ab}_{c}]_{\mu\nu}=[R^{ba}_{c}]_{\mu\nu}^{-1}
.

Changing the subjective choices in Eq. (
134
) gives a similar transformation as in Eq. (
24
). Since there are constraints on the moving operators, we require that the new moving operators do not violate the constraints. As such, for example, when the moving and splitting operators change according to Eq. (
21
), the
R^{ab}_{c}
transforms as

[R^{ab}_{c}]_{\mu\nu}\to\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}[R^{ab}_{c}]_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}[\Omega^{ba}_{c}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}[(\Omega^{ab}_{c})^{-1}]_{\nu^{\prime}\nu},

(137)

with
\Omega^{ab}_{c}=e^{i\phi^{b}_{12}}(\Omega_{0}(a,b;c))_{21}
. If one insists to make the supports of new
M^{a}_{01}
,
M^{b}_{41}
and
M^{a}_{12}
(
M^{b}_{12}
) operators sweep around
x_{1}
in a clockwise manner, then

[R^{ab}_{c}]_{\mu\nu}\to\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}[R^{ba}_{c}]^{-1}_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}[\Omega^{ba}_{c}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}[(\Omega^{ab}_{c})^{-1}]_{\nu^{\prime}\nu},

(138)

with
\Omega^{ab}_{c}=e^{i\phi^{b}_{12}}(\Omega_{0}(a,b;c))_{21}
. Moreover, in the Appendix B of Ref.
Kawagoe and Levin 2020
, it is proved that the
R
-matrices Eq. (
135
) satisfy the hexagon equations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{\lambda,\gamma}[R^{ac}_{e}]_{\alpha\lambda}[F^{acb}_{d}]_{(e,\lambda,\beta)(g,\gamma,\nu)}(R^{bc}_{g})_{\gamma\mu}&=\sum_{f,\sigma,\delta,\psi}[F^{cab}_{d}]_{\{e,\alpha,\beta\},\{f,\delta,\sigma\}}[R^{fc}_{d}]_{\sigma\psi}[F^{abc}_{d}]_{\{f,\delta,\psi\}),\{g,\mu,\nu\}},\\
\sum_{\lambda,\gamma}[(R^{ca}_{e})^{-1}]_{\alpha\gamma}[F^{acb}_{d}]_{\{e,\lambda,\beta\},\{g,\gamma,\nu\}}[(R^{cb}_{g})^{-1}]_{\gamma\mu}&=\sum_{f,\sigma,\delta,\mu}[F^{cab}_{d}]_{\{e,\alpha,\beta\},\{f,\delta,\sigma\}}[(R^{cf}_{d})^{-1}]_{\sigma\psi}[F^{abc}_{d}]_{\{f,\delta,\psi)(g,\mu,\nu\}}.\end{split}

(139)

From the above discussion, the consistency equations satisfied by these
F
- and
R
-matrices, and the equivalence relation they are subject to, make them valid
F
- and
R
-symbols in a braided tensor category.

Appendix G
The full structure of the universal data of symmetry-enriched TQSLs

In Sec.
IV
, we have introduced the universal data of symmetry-enriched TQSLs. The starting point there is the physical properties of TQSLs introduced in Secs.
III
and
IV
, based on which we have defined the data that characterizes the four features in TQSLs: 1) the associativity of fusion of anyons, 2) the braiding of anyons, 3) the change of fusion vertex basis states under the symmetry transformation, and 4) symmetry fractionalization. The general idea of defining this data is as follows. For each of the four features, we start with a certain 2-anyon state, perform two different physical processes that are related to this feature, and then take the inner product of the two final states. The two processes must consist of applications of only moving and splitting operators, symmetry transformations, and
V
operators, so that the results are related to the physics of anyons, rather than including irrelevant high-energy physics. The two resulting final states are required to differ by at most a unitary operation in the degenerate subspace of the locally indistinguishable fusion vertex basis states, so that the results can reflect nontrivial universal properties of the SET phases, rather than being completely arbitrary.

In Sec.
IV
, we focus on the data under the assumption that Eqs. (
11
), (
12
), and (
13
) hold. However, as we have emphasized, Eqs. (
11
), (
12
), and (
13
) are not necessary for defining the universal data. In this appendix, we discuss the full structure of the universal data. In particular, we introduce three parameters to quantify how Eqs. (
11
), (
12
), and (
13
) are violated: a phase factor
\gamma
resulting from the violation of Eq. (
11
); a unitary matrix
\Gamma
comparing the fusion vertex basis states at different positions, which quantifies the violation of Eq. (
12
); and a phase factor
\omega
resulting from the violation of Eq. (
13
). Concretely, given a set of anyon states, moving, splitting and
V
operators, the following relations hold:

\displaystyle M^{a}_{31}|a_{1},\bar{a}\rangle=\gamma^{a}_{321}M^{a}_{32}M^{a}_{21}|a_{1},\bar{a}\rangle;

(140)

\displaystyle(S^{ab}_{c;\mu})_{21}|c_{1},\bar{c}\rangle=\sum_{\mu^{\prime}}[(\Gamma^{ab}_{c})_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}21}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}M^{b}_{22^{\prime}}M^{a}_{11^{\prime}}(S^{ab}_{c;\mu^{\prime}})_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}}M^{c}_{1^{\prime}1}|c_{1},\bar{c}\rangle;

(141)

\displaystyle R_{g}M^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt1^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt2}R_{g}^{-1}V^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{g}(x_{2})|a_{2},\bar{a}\rangle=(\omega^{a}_{g})_{12}V^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{g}(x_{1})M^{a}_{12}|a_{2},\bar{a}\rangle.

(142)

The above relations are general. In particular, the two sides of Eq. (
11
) can only differ by a phase factor, and the two sides of Eq. (
12
) can only differ by a unitary matrix, because of the definitions of anyon states and the moving and splitting operators. The reason why the two sides of Eq. (
13
) can only differ by a phase factor is explained in Appendix
D
.

Note the notational difference of the above anyon states compared to the main text. For notational simplicity, just as above, we may omit the position index for the
\bar{a}
anyon in this appendix, because this anyon is far away from the region where we perform the various processes and is unimportant. Furthermore, in this appendix we will mostly use diagrammatic derivations. In particular, Eqs. (
140
), (
141
) and (
142
) can be expressed by the following diagrams.

Also note that the moving and splitting operators with different endpoints can be chosen to commute with each other in most cases. In the following, we will always make such a choice unless otherwise stated.

In the following, from Appendix
G.1
to Appendix
G.4
, we will consider a specific TQSL in the SET phase. In Appendix
G.5
, we will illustrate a graded structure of the universal data in the entire SET phase, where the three parameters
\gamma
,
\Gamma
, and
\omega
are the grading parameters.

This appendix is written in a largely self-contained manner, and many definitions given in the main text are also given here. In the following, we will sometimes talk about the categorical
F
-,
R
-,
U
- and
\eta
-symbols. The categorical
F
- and
R
-symbols can be viewed as the
F
- and
R
-matrices defined in Ref.
Kawagoe and Levin 2020
, which is also reviewed in Appendix
F
. These are also the
F-
and
R
-matrices defined in Eqs. (
16
) and (
19
), assuming Eqs. (
11
) and (
12
). The categorical
U
- and
\eta
-symbols can be viewed as the
U
-matrices and
\eta
-phases defined in Eqs. (
26
) and (
29
), assuming Eqs. (
11
), (
12
) and (
13
) and that the symmetry is purely internal.

G.1
The associativity of fusion:
F
-matrices

We begin with the first feature, i.e., the associativity of the fusion of anyons. The general process to capture this feature is as follows. We start from a certain anyon state
|d_{0},\bar{d}\rangle
with anyon
\bar{d}
far away from the region where the relevant processes are performed, split the anyon
d
into three anyons
a
,
b
, and
c
in two different ways, and compare the difference between these two processes by taking the inner product of the two final states. The processes considered below are the most general class of processes, in the sense that the splitting processes can happen at different locations, and the anyons are allowed to move freely at every stage before they reach the final state. At the same time, these processes are the simplest ones in these most general processes, in the sense that the motion of anyons before and after each splitting process is achieved by applying a single moving operator, rather than multiple moving operators.

According to this general idea, the associativity of fusion is captured by

[(F^{abc}_{d})_{012341^{\prime}2^{\prime}3^{\prime}4^{\prime}567}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\})=\langle f,\rho,\sigma|e,\mu,\nu\rangle,

(143)

where

\displaystyle|e,\mu,\nu\rangle=M^{b}_{64}M^{a}_{52}(S^{ab}_{e,\mu})_{42}M^{e}_{21}M^{c}_{73}(S^{ec}_{d,\nu})_{31}M^{d}_{10}|d_{0},\bar{d}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle,

(144)

\displaystyle|f,\rho,\sigma\rangle=M^{c}_{74^{\prime}}M^{b}_{63^{\prime}}(S^{bc}_{f,\rho})_{4^{\prime}3^{\prime}}M^{f}_{3^{\prime}2^{\prime}}M^{a}_{51^{\prime}}(S^{af}_{d,\sigma})_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}}M^{d}_{1^{\prime}0}|d_{0},\bar{d}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle.

(145)

As we can see from the definition,
F
-matrices have explicit dependence on the choice of anyon states collectively represented by
\{|\psi\rangle\}
, the choice of the moving and splitting operators collectively represented by
\{M\}
and
\{S\}
, respectively, and 12 positions collectively represented by
012341^{\prime}2^{\prime}3^{\prime}4^{\prime}567
. Below we will sometimes write the position labels as
\{x\}
for simplicity. All moving and splitting operators in the above processes can be chosen to commute with each other as long as they have different endpoints.

If
x_{0}
,
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
,
x_{6}
,
x_{1^{\prime}}
and
x_{3^{\prime}}
are all the same position, and
x_{3}
,
x_{4}
,
x_{2^{\prime}}
and
x_{4^{\prime}}
are also all the same position, then the processes that define the above
F
-matrix are precisely the same as the protocol defined by Kawagoe and Levin
Kawagoe and Levin 2020
(see Appendix
F
for a review). In particular, when
x_{0}
,
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
,
x_{6}
,
x_{1^{\prime}}
,
x_{3^{\prime}}
are identified as a single position, the moving operators between them become phase factors that can be absorbed into some splitting operators. Similar statement is true when
x_{3}
,
x_{4}
,
x_{2^{\prime}}
and
x_{4^{\prime}}
are identified as a single position. Therefore, the Kawagoe-Levin definition of
F
-matrices, being the categorical
F
-symbols, are special cases of the microscopic
F
-matrices in Eq. (
143
).

On the other hand, in general, the
F
-matrix defined in Eq. (
143
) is different from the Kawagoe-Levin
F
-matrix in Ref.
Kawagoe and Levin 2020
. Below we discuss the properties of the general microscopic
F
-matrices. There are two questions to be answered. First, what is the relation between the
F
-matrix in Eq. (
143
) and the categorical
F
-symbol? Given the pentagon equation Eq. (
17
) satisfied by the categorical
F
-symbol, this relation allows us to write down an analogous equation satisfied by the
F
-matrix in Eq. (
143
). Second, since the
F
-matrix in Eq. (
143
) has explicit dependence on the choices of
\{|\psi\rangle\}
,
\{M\}
and
\{S\}
, how does it transform when these choices change? The answer to this question allows us to understand the equivalence class of the
F
-matrices.

G.1.1
The relation between microscopic
F
-matrices and categorical
F
-symbols

The strategy to answer the first question is to compare the above definition, Eq. (
143
), with the
F
-matrices defined in Ref.
Kawagoe and Levin 2020
and Appendix
F
. We pick up four positions
\tilde{x}_{0}
,
\tilde{x}_{1}
,
\tilde{x}_{2}
, and
\tilde{x}_{3}
to extract the
F
-symbols using the Kawagoe-Levin formalism; these
F
-symbols are denoted by
[(\tilde{F}^{abc}_{d})_{\tilde{0}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}\tilde{3}}]_{\{e,\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}\},\{f,\rho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}\}}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\})=\widetilde{\langle f,\rho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}|}\widetilde{e,\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}\rangle}
in the following, where
\widetilde{|f,\rho,\sigma\rangle}
and
\widetilde{|e,\mu,\nu\rangle}
are defined via the process introduced in Eq. (
130
). We also find
12
positions
x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{1^{\prime}},x_{2^{\prime}},x_{3^{\prime}},x_{4^{\prime}},x_{5},x_{6},x_{7}
to extract the
F
-matrices defined in Eq. (
143
). We can find the relation between
|e,\mu,\nu\rangle
and
\widetilde{|e,\mu,\nu\rangle}
, and
|f,\rho,\sigma\rangle
and
\widetilde{|f,\rho,\sigma\rangle}
, depicted as follows.

An important feature of the relation Eq. (
149
) is that it
does not
depend on the choice of the moving operators
M_{x\tilde{x}}
connecting
x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{1^{\prime}},x_{2^{\prime}},x_{3^{\prime}},x_{4^{\prime}},x_{5},x_{6},x_{7}
and
\tilde{x}_{0},\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}
, which is expected because the explicit definitions of the
F
-matrix in Eq. (
143
) and the categorical
\tilde{F}
-symbol in Eq. (
131
) do not involve those moving operators. To verify this property, we just need to verify that Eq. (
149
) is invariant even if we redefine these moving operators. Under this redefinition, according to Eq. (
21
), the
\gamma

\Gamma
matrices transform as

\displaystyle\gamma^{a}_{2\tilde{2}\tilde{1}1}\to e^{-i\phi_{\tilde{1}1}(a)}e^{-i\phi_{2\tilde{2}}(a)}\gamma^{a}_{2\tilde{2}\tilde{1}1},\quad\gamma^{a}_{2\tilde{2}1}\to e^{-i\phi_{\tilde{2}1}(a)}e^{-i\phi_{2\tilde{2}}(a)}\gamma^{a}_{2\tilde{2}1},

\displaystyle[(\Gamma^{ab}_{c})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}21}]_{\mu\nu}\to e^{-i\phi_{\tilde{1}1}(c)}e^{-i\phi_{1\tilde{1}}(a)}e^{-i\phi_{2\tilde{2}}(b)}[(\Gamma^{ab}_{c})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}21}]_{\mu\nu}.

Substituting the above re-definition into Eq. (
149
), it is straightforward to show that Eq. (
149
) is invariant.

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{7_{4}\tilde{3}\tilde{2}4_{4}^{\prime}}^{d}\gamma_{6_{4}\tilde{1}3_{4}^{\prime}}^{h}\gamma_{3_{4}^{\prime}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}2_{4}^{\prime}}^{k}\gamma_{5_{4}\tilde{0}\tilde{1}1_{4}^{\prime}}^{a}\gamma_{1_{4}^{\prime}\tilde{1}0_{4}}^{e}}{\gamma^{h}_{6_{4}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}4_{4}}\gamma^{a}_{5_{4}\tilde{0}\tilde{1}2_{4}}\gamma^{g}_{2_{4}\tilde{1}1_{4}}\gamma^{d}_{7_{4}\tilde{3}\tilde{2}3_{4}}\gamma^{g}_{1_{4}\tilde{1}0_{4}}}\times\frac{\gamma_{\tilde{2}4_{4}\tilde{2}}^{d}\gamma_{\tilde{1}2_{4}\tilde{1}}^{a}\gamma_{\tilde{1}2_{4}\tilde{1}}^{g}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{4}\tilde{1}}^{g}\gamma_{\tilde{2}3_{4}\tilde{2}}^{d}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{4}\tilde{1}}^{e}}{\gamma_{\tilde{2}4_{4}^{\prime}\tilde{2}}^{d}\gamma_{\tilde{1}3_{4}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{d}\gamma_{\tilde{1}3_{4}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{k}\gamma_{\tilde{2}2_{4}^{\prime}\tilde{2}}^{k}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{4}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{a}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{4}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{e}}\times

\displaystyle[(\Gamma^{bc}_{h})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}4_{5}2_{5}}]_{\varphi\iota^{\prime}}^{-1}[(\Gamma^{hd}_{k})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}3_{5}1_{5}}]_{\rho\zeta^{\prime}}^{-1}[(F^{bcd}_{k})_{\{x_{5}\}}]_{\{h,\iota^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime}\},\{l,\nu^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}\}}[(\Gamma_{l}^{cd})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}4_{5}^{\prime}3_{5}^{\prime}}]_{\nu^{\prime}\nu}[(\Gamma_{k}^{hl})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}2_{5}^{\prime}1_{5}^{\prime}}]_{\mu^{\prime}\mu}\times

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{7_{5}\tilde{3}\tilde{2}4_{5}^{\prime}}^{d}\gamma_{6_{5}\tilde{1}3_{5}^{\prime}}^{c}\gamma_{3_{5}^{\prime}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}2_{5}^{\prime}}^{l}\gamma_{5_{5}\tilde{0}\tilde{1}1_{5}^{\prime}}^{b}\gamma_{1_{5}^{\prime}\tilde{1}0_{5}}^{k}}{\gamma^{c}_{6_{5}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}4_{5}}\gamma^{h}_{5_{5}\tilde{0}\tilde{1}2_{5}}\gamma^{b}_{2_{5}\tilde{1}1_{5}}\gamma^{d}_{7_{5}\tilde{3}\tilde{2}3_{5}}\gamma^{h}_{1_{5}\tilde{1}0_{5}}}\times\frac{\gamma_{\tilde{2}4_{5}\tilde{2}}^{d}\gamma_{\tilde{1}2_{5}\tilde{1}}^{b}\gamma_{\tilde{1}2_{5}\tilde{1}}^{h}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{5}\tilde{1}}^{h}\gamma_{\tilde{2}3_{5}\tilde{2}}^{d}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{5}\tilde{1}}^{k}}{\gamma_{\tilde{2}4_{5}^{\prime}\tilde{2}}^{d}\gamma_{\tilde{1}3_{5}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{d}\gamma_{\tilde{1}3_{5}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{l}\gamma_{\tilde{2}2_{5}^{\prime}\tilde{2}}^{l}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{5}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{b}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{5}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{k}}.

The above equation involves
F
-matrices defined at five different sets of positions, with each set containing
12
positions. When the
F
-matrices coincide with category
F
-symbols, which is the case when Eqs. (
11
) and (
12
) hold, it is straightforward to check that the above equation reduces to the pentagon identity in Eq. (
17
).

G.1.2
The microscopic transformations of
F
-matrices

Now we answer the second question: how does the
F
-matrix in Eq. (
143
) transform when
\{|\psi\rangle\}
,
M
and
S
change? Understanding this question amounts to understanding how
F
-matrices vary under the change of the following choices: the choice of moving and splitting operators, the choice of the local information of the initial anyon states, and choice of the positions of the anyons used to define
F
-matrix. Below we analyze the effects of these three types of changes in turn.

•

Changing the moving and splitting operators used to define the
F
-matrix.

Under this change, Eq. (
21
) gives the allowed redefinition of the moving and splitting operators. Under such transformations, the two states
|e,\mu,\nu\rangle
and
|f,\rho,\sigma\rangle
change as

\displaystyle|e,\mu,\nu\rangle\to\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}e^{i\phi_{52}(a)}e^{i\phi_{64}(b)}e^{i\phi_{73}(c)}[\Omega_{0}(a,b;e)_{42}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}e^{i\phi_{21}(e)}[\Omega_{0}(e,c;d)_{31}]_{\nu\nu^{\prime}}e^{i\phi_{10}(d)}|e,\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}\rangle;

\displaystyle|f,\rho,\sigma\rangle\to\sum_{\rho^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}e^{i\phi_{52^{\prime}}(a)}e^{i\phi_{64^{\prime}}(b)}e^{i\phi_{73^{\prime}}(c)}[\Omega_{0}(b,c;f)_{4^{\prime}3^{\prime}}]_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}e^{i\phi_{3^{\prime}2^{\prime}}(f)}[\Omega_{0}(a,f;d)_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}}]_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}e^{i\phi_{1^{\prime}0}(d)}|f,\rho,\sigma\rangle.

As such, the
F
-matrix in Eq. (
143
) transforms as

\displaystyle[(F^{abc}_{d})_{\{x\}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\})

(151)

\displaystyle\to\frac{e^{i\phi_{52}(a)}e^{i\phi_{64}(b)}e^{i\phi_{73}(c)}e^{i\phi_{21}(e)}e^{i\phi_{10}(d)}}{e^{i\phi_{51^{\prime}}(a)}e^{i\phi_{63^{\prime}}(b)}e^{i\phi_{74^{\prime}}(c)}e^{i\phi_{3^{\prime}2^{\prime}}(f)}e^{i\phi_{1^{\prime}0}(d)}}\cdot\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}

\displaystyle[\Omega_{0}(a,b;e)_{42}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}[\Omega_{0}(e,c;d)_{31}]_{\nu\nu^{\prime}}[(F^{abc}_{d})_{\{x\}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu^{\prime}\},\{f,\rho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}\}}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\})[\Omega_{0}(b,c;f)_{4^{\prime}3^{\prime}}]_{\rho^{\prime}\rho}^{-1}[\Omega_{0}(a,f;d)_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}}]_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}^{-1}.

Notably, when
[(F^{abc}_{d})_{012341^{\prime}2^{\prime}3^{\prime}4^{\prime}567}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}
is simplified to the Kawagoe-Levin definition, i.e., when
\{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{6},x_{1^{\prime}},x_{3^{\prime}}\}
are identified with
\tilde{x}_{1}
,
\{x_{3},x_{4},x_{2^{\prime}},x_{4^{\prime}}\}
are identified with
\tilde{x}_{2}
,
x_{5}
is identified with
\tilde{x}_{0}
, and
x_{7}
is identified with
\tilde{x}_{3}
, the above transformation is simplified to

\displaystyle[(F^{abc}_{d})_{\tilde{0}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}\tilde{3}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\})\to\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}\left\{e^{i\phi_{\tilde{1}\tilde{2}}(b)}[\Omega_{0}(a,b;e)_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\right\}\left\{e^{i\phi_{\tilde{1}\tilde{2}}(c)}[\Omega_{0}(e,c;d)_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}}]_{\nu\nu^{\prime}}\right\}\times

\displaystyle[(F^{abc}_{d})_{\tilde{0}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}\tilde{3}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu^{\prime}\},\{f,\rho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}\}}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\})\left\{e^{i\phi_{\tilde{1}\tilde{2}}(c)}[\Omega_{0}(b,c;f)_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}}]_{\rho^{\prime}\rho}\right\}^{-1}\left\{e^{i\phi_{\tilde{1}\tilde{2}}(f)}[\Omega_{0}(a,f;d)_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}}]_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}\right\}^{-1}.

This simplified transformation is also introduced in Ref.
Kawagoe and Levin 2020
, which takes precisely the same form as the vertex basis gauge transformation of the categorical
\tilde{F}
-symbols.

•

Changing the local information of the chosen anyon states.

Suppose we change the definition of anyon states, such that the anyons have different local information. According to our discussions in Sec.
III
, the new anyon states can be obtained by applying unitary operators localized around the anyons, i.e., the anyon states transform as

|a_{1},b_{2},\cdots\rangle\to W(x_{1})W({x_{2}})\cdots|a_{1},b_{2},\cdots\rangle=W(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots)|a_{1},b_{2},\cdots\rangle,

where
W(x_{i})
is a unitary operator localized around the position
x_{i}
, and
W(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots)=\prod_{i}W(x_{i})
. Correspondingly, the moving and splitting operators should also change according to their definitions in Eq. (
3
) and Eq. (
4
). In particular, we can always make the following choice:

\displaystyle M^{a}_{21}\to W(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots)M^{a}_{21}W(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots)^{\dagger},

(152)

\displaystyle(S^{ab}_{c,\mu})_{21}\to W(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots)(S^{ab}_{c,\mu})_{21}W(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots)^{\dagger}.

Note that the new moving and splitting operators still have their supports around their corresponding old operators. As such, the final states
|e,\mu,\nu\rangle
and
|f,\rho,\sigma\rangle
are changed to
W(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots)|e,\mu,\nu\rangle
and
W(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots)|f,\rho,\sigma\rangle
, and their inner product does not change. Hence, the microscopic
F
-matrix stays invariant when we change the local information of the anyon states and choose these particular moving and splitting operators. Certainly, we can also make other choices of moving and splitting operators. Then the
F
-matrix will undergo a transformation triggered by changing the definition of moving and splitting operators, which is given in Eq. (
151
).

•

Changing the position of anyons.

Since the
F
-matrices have explicit dependence on the positions of anyons, they are also expected to change when we calculate them at different positions. In particular,
[(F^{abc}_{d})_{0_{1}1_{1}2_{1}3_{1}4_{1}{1_{1}^{\prime}}{2_{1}^{\prime}}3_{1}^{\prime}4_{1}^{\prime}5_{1}6_{1}7_{1}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}
will be different from
[(F^{abc}_{d})_{0_{2}1_{2}2_{2}3_{2}4_{2}{1_{2}^{\prime}}{2_{2}^{\prime}}3_{2}^{\prime}4_{2}^{\prime}5_{2}6_{2}7_{2}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}
, where the positions with subscripts 1 and 2 are two sets of positions, respectively. The strategy to find the relation between them is to relate them to a set of common categorical
F
-symbols extracted at fixed positions
\tilde{x}_{0}
,
\tilde{x}_{1}
,
\tilde{x}_{2}
and
\tilde{x}_{3}
. According to Eq. (
149
), we have (in the following equation, we have made the position dependence explicit and hide the dependence on the anyon states and the moving and splitting operators.)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}[(\Gamma^{ab}_{e})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}4_{1}2_{1}}]_{\mu^{\prime}\mu}^{-1}[(\Gamma^{ec}_{d})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}3_{1}1_{1}}]_{\nu^{\prime}\nu}^{-1}[(F^{abc}_{d})_{0_{1}1_{1}2_{1}3_{1}4_{1}{1_{1}^{\prime}}{2_{1}^{\prime}}3_{1}^{\prime}4_{1}^{\prime}5_{1}6_{1}7_{1}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}\;[(\Gamma_{f}^{bc})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}4_{1}^{\prime}3_{1}^{\prime}}]_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}[(\Gamma_{d}^{af})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}2_{1}^{\prime}1_{1}^{\prime}}]_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}\times

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{5_{1}\tilde{3}\tilde{2}4_{1}^{\prime}}^{c}\gamma_{6_{1}\tilde{1}3_{1}^{\prime}}^{b}\gamma_{3_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}2_{1}^{\prime}}^{f}\gamma_{5_{1}\tilde{0}\tilde{1}1_{1}^{\prime}}^{a}\gamma_{1_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{1}0_{1}}^{d}}{\gamma^{b}_{6_{1}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}4_{1}}\gamma^{a}_{5_{1}\tilde{0}\tilde{1}2_{1}}\gamma^{e}_{2_{1}\tilde{1}1_{1}}\gamma^{c}_{7_{1}\tilde{3}\tilde{2}3_{1}}\gamma^{d}_{1_{1}\tilde{1}0}}\times\frac{\gamma_{\tilde{2}4_{1}\tilde{2}}^{b}\gamma_{\tilde{1}2_{1}\tilde{1}}^{a}\gamma_{\tilde{1}2_{1}\tilde{1}}^{e}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{1}\tilde{1}}^{e}\gamma_{\tilde{2}3_{1}\tilde{2}}^{c}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{1}\tilde{1}}^{d}}{\gamma_{\tilde{2}4_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{2}}^{c}\gamma_{\tilde{1}3_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{b}\gamma_{\tilde{1}3_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{f}\gamma_{\tilde{2}2_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{2}}^{f}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{a}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{d}}=[(\tilde{F}^{abc}_{d})_{\tilde{0}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}\tilde{3}}]_{\{e,\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}\},\{f,\rho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}\}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\beta\lambda\kappa}[(\Gamma^{ab}_{e})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}4_{2}2_{2}}]_{\mu^{\prime}\alpha}^{-1}[(\Gamma^{ec}_{d})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}3_{2}1_{2}}]_{\nu^{\prime}\beta}^{-1}[(F^{abc}_{d})_{0_{2}1_{2}2_{2}3_{2}4_{2}{1_{2}^{\prime}}{2_{2}^{\prime}}3_{2}^{\prime}4_{2}^{\prime}5_{2}6_{2}7_{2}}]_{\{e,\alpha,\beta\},\{f,\lambda,\kappa\}}\;[(\Gamma_{f}^{bc})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}4_{2}^{\prime}3_{2}^{\prime}}]_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}[(\Gamma_{d}^{af})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}2_{2}^{\prime}1_{2}^{\prime}}]_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}\times

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{5_{2}\tilde{3}\tilde{2}4_{2}^{\prime}}^{c}\gamma_{6_{2}\tilde{1}3_{2}^{\prime}}^{b}\gamma_{3_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}2_{2}^{\prime}}^{f}\gamma_{5_{2}\tilde{0}\tilde{1}1_{2}^{\prime}}^{a}\gamma_{1_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{1}0_{2}}^{d}}{\gamma^{b}_{6_{2}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}4_{2}}\gamma^{a}_{5_{2}\tilde{0}\tilde{1}2_{2}}\gamma^{e}_{2_{2}\tilde{1}1_{2}}\gamma^{c}_{7_{2}\tilde{3}\tilde{2}3_{2}}\gamma^{d}_{1_{2}\tilde{1}0}}\times\frac{\gamma_{\tilde{2}4_{2}\tilde{2}}^{b}\gamma_{\tilde{1}2_{2}\tilde{1}}^{a}\gamma_{\tilde{1}2_{2}\tilde{1}}^{e}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{2}\tilde{1}}^{e}\gamma_{\tilde{2}3_{2}\tilde{2}}^{c}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{2}\tilde{1}}^{d}}{\gamma_{\tilde{2}4_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{2}}^{c}\gamma_{\tilde{1}3_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{b}\gamma_{\tilde{1}3_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{f}\gamma_{\tilde{2}2_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{2}}^{f}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{a}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{d}}.

Consequently, the
F
-matrices defined at two different sets of positions are related by

\displaystyle[(F^{abc}_{d})_{0_{1}1_{1}2_{1}3_{1}4_{1}{1_{1}^{\prime}}{2_{1}^{\prime}}3_{1}^{\prime}4_{1}^{\prime}5_{1}6_{1}7_{1}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}=\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}\sum_{\alpha\beta\lambda\kappa}\frac{\gamma^{b}_{6_{1}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}4_{1}}}{\gamma^{b}_{6_{2}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}4_{2}}}\frac{\gamma^{a}_{5_{1}\tilde{0}\tilde{1}2_{1}}}{\gamma^{a}_{5_{2}\tilde{0}\tilde{1}2_{2}}}\left\{[(\Gamma^{ab}_{e})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}4_{1}2_{1}}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}[(\Gamma^{ab}_{e})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}4_{2}2_{2}}]_{\mu^{\prime}\alpha}^{-1}\right\}\frac{\gamma^{e}_{2_{1}\tilde{1}1_{1}}}{\gamma^{e}_{2_{2}\tilde{1}1_{2}}}\frac{\gamma^{c}_{7_{1}\tilde{3}\tilde{2}3_{1}}}{\gamma^{c}_{7_{2}\tilde{3}\tilde{2}3_{2}}}

(153)

\displaystyle\left\{[(\Gamma^{ec}_{d})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}3_{1}1_{1}}]_{\nu\beta}[(\Gamma^{ec}_{d})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}3_{2}1_{2}}]_{\nu^{\prime}\beta}^{-1}\right\}\frac{\gamma^{d}_{1_{1}\tilde{1}0_{1}}}{\gamma^{d}_{1_{2}\tilde{1}0_{2}}}\times[(F^{abc}_{d})_{0_{2}1_{2}2_{2}3_{2}4_{2}{1_{2}^{\prime}}{2_{2}^{\prime}}3_{2}^{\prime}4_{2}^{\prime}5_{2}6_{2}7_{2}}]_{\{e,\alpha,\beta\},\{f,\lambda,\kappa\}}\times

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{7_{2}\tilde{3}\tilde{2}4_{2}^{\prime}}^{c}}{\gamma_{7_{1}\tilde{3}\tilde{2}4_{1}^{\prime}}^{c}}\frac{\gamma_{6_{2}\tilde{1}3_{2}^{\prime}}^{b}}{\gamma_{6_{1}\tilde{1}3_{1}^{\prime}}^{b}}\left\{[(\Gamma_{f}^{bc})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}4_{1}^{\prime}3_{1}^{\prime}}]_{\lambda\rho}^{-1}[(\Gamma_{f}^{bc})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}4_{2}^{\prime}3_{2}^{\prime}}]_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}\right\}\frac{\gamma_{3_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}2_{2}^{\prime}}^{f}}{\gamma_{3_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}2_{1}^{\prime}}^{f}}\frac{\gamma_{5_{2}\tilde{0}\tilde{1}1_{2}^{\prime}}^{a}}{\gamma_{5_{1}\tilde{0}\tilde{1}1_{1}^{\prime}}^{a}}\left\{[(\Gamma_{d}^{af})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}2_{1}^{\prime}1_{1}^{\prime}}]_{\kappa\sigma}^{-1}[(\Gamma_{d}^{af})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}2_{2}^{\prime}1_{2}^{\prime}}]_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}\right\}\frac{\gamma_{1_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{1}0_{2}}^{d}}{\gamma_{1_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{1}0_{1}}^{d}}\times

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{\tilde{2}4_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{2}}^{c}\gamma_{\tilde{1}3_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{b}\gamma_{\tilde{1}3_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{f}\gamma_{\tilde{2}2_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{2}}^{f}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{a}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{d}}{\gamma_{\tilde{2}4_{1}\tilde{2}}^{b}\gamma_{\tilde{1}2_{1}\tilde{1}}^{a}\gamma_{\tilde{1}2_{1}\tilde{1}}^{e}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{1}\tilde{1}}^{e}\gamma_{\tilde{2}3_{1}\tilde{2}}^{c}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{1}\tilde{1}}^{d}}\times\frac{\gamma_{\tilde{2}4_{2}\tilde{2}}^{b}\gamma_{\tilde{1}2_{2}\tilde{1}}^{a}\gamma_{\tilde{1}2_{2}\tilde{1}}^{e}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{2}\tilde{1}}^{e}\gamma_{\tilde{2}3_{2}\tilde{2}}^{c}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{2}\tilde{1}}^{d}}{\gamma_{\tilde{2}4_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{2}}^{c}\gamma_{\tilde{1}3_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{b}\gamma_{\tilde{1}3_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{f}\gamma_{\tilde{2}2_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{2}}^{f}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{a}\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{2}^{\prime}\tilde{1}}^{d}}.

Although the phase factors and matrices in the above relation have explicit dependence on the choice of the position
\tilde{x}_{0},\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}
, the moving and splitting operators involving only
\tilde{x}_{0},\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}
, the moving operators between
\tilde{x}_{0},\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}
and
x_{0_{1}},x_{1_{1}},x_{2_{1}},x_{3_{1}},x_{4_{1}},x_{1_{1}^{\prime}},x_{2_{1}^{\prime}},x_{3_{1}^{\prime}},x_{4_{1}^{\prime}},x_{5_{1}},x_{6_{1}},x_{7_{1}}
, and the moving operators between
\tilde{x}_{0},\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}
and
x_{0_{2}},x_{1_{2}},x_{2_{2}},x_{3_{2}},x_{4_{2}},x_{1_{2}^{\prime}},x_{2_{2}^{\prime}},x_{3_{2}^{\prime}},x_{4_{2}^{\prime}},x_{5_{2}},x_{6_{2}},x_{7_{2}}
, the combination of these phases and matrices is independent of these choices, which is expected because
[(F^{abc}_{d})_{0_{1}1_{1}2_{1}3_{1}4_{1}{1_{1}^{\prime}}{2_{1}^{\prime}}3_{1}^{\prime}4_{1}^{\prime}5_{1}6_{1}7_{1}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}
being different from
[(F^{abc}_{d})_{0_{2}1_{2}2_{2}3_{2}4_{2}{1_{2}^{\prime}}{2_{2}^{\prime}}3_{2}^{\prime}4_{2}^{\prime}5_{2}6_{2}7_{2}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}
does not depend on them. This independence can be seen by picking up other
\{\tilde{x}\}
and re-defining these moving and splitting operators, which will result in precisely the same expression. Again, when both of
[(F^{abc}_{d})_{0_{1}1_{1}2_{1}3_{1}4_{1}{1_{1}^{\prime}}{2_{1}^{\prime}}3_{1}^{\prime}4_{1}^{\prime}5_{1}6_{1}7_{1}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}
and
[(F^{abc}_{d})_{0_{2}1_{2}2_{2}3_{2}4_{2}{1_{2}^{\prime}}{2_{2}^{\prime}}3_{2}^{\prime}4_{2}^{\prime}5_{2}6_{2}7_{2}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}
are simplified to the Kawagoe-Levin definition, which gives
[(\tilde{F}^{abc}_{d})_{\{0_{1}1_{1}2_{1}3_{1}\}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}
and
[(\tilde{F}^{abc}_{d})_{\{0_{2}1_{2}2_{2}3_{2}\}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}
, respectively, the above transformation is greatly simplified into

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &[(\tilde{F}^{abc}_{d})_{0_{1}1_{1}2_{1}3_{1}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}\\
=&\sum_{\alpha\beta\lambda\kappa}[(\tilde{\Gamma}^{ab}_{e})_{2_{2}1_{2}2_{1}1_{1}}]_{\mu\alpha}[(\tilde{\Gamma}^{ec}_{d})_{2_{2}1_{2}2_{1}1_{1}}]_{\nu\beta}[(\tilde{F}^{abc}_{d})_{0_{2}1_{2}2_{2}3_{2}}]_{\{e,\alpha,\beta\},\{f,\lambda,\kappa\}}[(\tilde{\Gamma}^{bc}_{f})_{2_{2}1_{2}2_{1}1_{1}}]_{\lambda\rho}[(\tilde{\Gamma}^{af}_{d})_{2_{2}1_{2}2_{1}1_{1}}]_{\kappa\sigma},\end{aligned}

(154)

where

[(\tilde{\Gamma}^{ab}_{c})_{2_{2}1_{2}2_{1}1_{1}}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}=\sum_{\mu^{\prime}}\left\{[(\Gamma^{ab}_{c})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}2_{1}1_{1}}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}[(\Gamma^{ab}_{c})_{\tilde{2}\tilde{1}2_{2}1_{1}}]_{\mu^{\prime}\alpha}^{-1}\right\}\frac{\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{2}\tilde{1}}^{a}}{\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{1}\tilde{1}}^{a}}\frac{\gamma_{1_{1}\tilde{1}1_{1}}^{a}}{\gamma_{1_{2}\tilde{1}1_{2}}^{a}}\frac{\gamma^{b}_{1_{1}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}2_{1}}}{\gamma^{b}_{1_{2}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}2_{2}}}\frac{\gamma_{\tilde{2}2_{2}\tilde{2}}^{b}}{\gamma_{\tilde{2}2_{1}\tilde{2}}^{b}}\frac{\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{2}\tilde{1}}^{e}}{\gamma_{\tilde{1}1_{1}\tilde{1}}^{e}}.

This transformation is again the vertex basis gauge transformation of the categorical
F
-symbols.

To summarize, when we change the moving and splitting operators, and/or change the local information of the anyons, the
F
-matrix changes according to Eq. (
151
). When we change the positions of anyons used to define the
F
-matrix, the
F
-matrix undergoes a transformation given by Eq. (
153
). When Eqs. (
11
) and (
12
) hold, Eqs. (
151
) and (
153
) take the form of Eq. (
24
).

G.2
The braiding of anyons:
R
-matrices

The second characteristic feature of topological order is the braiding of anyons. Roughly speaking, the braiding property of two anyons is contained in the process of exchanging the positions of the two anyons
a
and
b
. In real experiments, the exchange process may not be precise in general, namely the anyon
a
may not be moved to exactly the original position of
b
, but some position close to the original position of
b
instead. Therefore, we define the general process of braiding as follows. We use two orthogonal strips to divide the space into four regions, as depicted in Fig.
9
(a). The strips must be wide enough to contain the support of anyon moving and splitting operators, and there is no upper limit for the width. The four regions II-I-III-IV should wind around the overlapping region of the strip in a counter-clockwise manner. We find two points
x_{1}
and
x_{1^{\prime}}
in region I, two points
x_{2}
and
x_{2^{\prime}}
in region II, one point
x_{3}
in region III, one point
x_{4}
in region IV, and one point
x_{0}
without any constraint on its position. There is no constraint on the relative position of
x_{1}
and
x_{1^{\prime}}
(
x_{2}
and
x_{2^{\prime}}
), either. The
R
-matrices that encodes the braiding is defined by

[(R^{ab}_{c})_{0121^{\prime}2^{\prime}34}]_{\mu\nu}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\})=\langle\nu|\mu\rangle,

(155)

where

\displaystyle|\mu\rangle=M^{a}_{32}M^{b}_{41}(S^{ba}_{c,\mu})_{21}M^{c}_{10}|c_{0},\bar{c}\rangle,

(156)

\displaystyle|\nu\rangle=M^{b}_{42^{\prime}}M^{a}_{31^{\prime}}(S^{ab}_{c,\nu})_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}}M^{c}_{1^{\prime}0}|c_{0},\bar{c}\rangle.

There are also some requirements on the moving operators. The support of the splitting operator
(S^{ba}_{c})_{21}
and
(S^{ab}_{c})_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}}
should not overlap with regions III and IV; the support of the moving operator
M^{b}_{41}
(
M^{a}_{32}
) is required to not overlap with regions II and III (regions I and IV); the support of the moving operator
M^{b}_{42^{\prime}}
(
M^{a}_{31^{\prime}}
) should not overlap with regions I and III (regions II and IV). As such, the two processes in Eq. (
155
) differ by an effect of changing the anyon positions in a counter-clockwise direction in a rough sense.

Similar to the processes that define the
F
-matrix in Eq. (
143
), the processes considered here are the simplest among the most general braiding processes. Namely, they are general since the splitting processes can happen at different locations, and the anyons can move before and after the splitting processes. At the same time, they are simple since the motion of anyons before and after each splitting process is achieved by applying a single moving operator, rather than multiple moving operators.

Below we discuss the properties of the
R
-matrices. We again answer the two questions: 1) What is the relation between this microscopic
R
-matrix in Eq. (
155
) and the Kawagoe-Levin
R
-symbol in Eq. (
135
)? And 2) how does the
R
-matrix transform when its microscopic input in Eq. (
155
), including the moving and splitting operators, and the local information and positions of anyons, changes?

G.2.1
The relation between microscopic
R
-matrices and categorical
R
-symbols

To understand the relation between the microscopic
R
-matrix in Eq. (
155
) and the categorical
R
-symbol in Eq. (
135
), which is denoted by
\tilde{R}
in this appendix, we first consider a simplified situation, where
x_{1}
and
x_{1^{\prime}}
are the same position denoted by
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
and
x_{2^{\prime}}
are the same position denoted by
x_{2}
, identifying
M^{a}_{32}
with
M^{a}_{31}M^{a}_{12}
, identifying
M^{c}_{1^{\prime}0}
with
M^{c}_{1^{\prime}1}M^{c}_{10}
, identifying
M^{b}_{42^{\prime}}
with
M^{b}_{41}M^{b}_{12}M^{b}_{22^{\prime}}
, and identifying
M^{a}_{31^{\prime}}
with
M^{a}_{31}M^{a}_{11^{\prime}}
. Figure
10
shows the identification of the moving operators and the support of the new moving operators. The first two identifications amount to making specific choices of anyon positions, and the last three identifications fix
\gamma^{a}_{312}=\gamma^{c}_{1^{\prime}10}=\gamma^{b}_{4122^{\prime}}=\gamma^{a}_{311^{\prime}}=1
. Also, the support of the moving operator
M^{a}_{31}
(
M^{a}_{12}
) is required to not overlap with regions II and IV (regions III and IV), the support of
M^{b}_{12}
is required to not overlap with regions III and IV, the support of
M^{a}_{11^{\prime}}
(
M^{b}_{22^{\prime}}
) is required to not overlap with regions II, III, and IV (regions I, III, and IV), and there is no constraint on the support of
M^{c}_{1^{\prime}1}
. After the simplification, the definition of
R
-matrices becomes identical to the Kawagoe-Levin definition based on the initial state
M^{c}_{30}|c_{0},\bar{c}\rangle
. Thus, we can use Eqs. (
140
) and (
141
) to find the relation between
R
and
\tilde{R}
. In particular,

\displaystyle|\mu\rangle=\gamma^{a}_{312}M^{a}_{31}M^{a}_{12}M^{b}_{41}(S^{ba}_{c,\mu})_{21}M^{c}_{10}|c_{0},\bar{c}\rangle,

(157)

\displaystyle|\nu\rangle=\gamma^{b}_{4122^{\prime}}\gamma^{c}_{1^{\prime}10}\gamma^{a}_{311^{\prime}}M^{b}_{41}M^{b}_{12}M^{b}_{22^{\prime}}M^{a}_{31}M^{a}_{11^{\prime}}(S^{ab}_{c,\nu})_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}}M^{c}_{1^{\prime}1}M^{c}_{10}|c_{0},\bar{c}\rangle

\displaystyle=\gamma^{b}_{4122^{\prime}}\gamma^{c}_{1^{\prime}10}\gamma^{a}_{311^{\prime}}[(\Gamma^{ab}_{c})_{212^{\prime}1^{\prime}}]_{\nu\nu^{\prime}}^{-1}M^{b}_{41}M^{b}_{12}M^{a}_{31}(S^{ab}_{c,\nu})_{21}M^{c}_{10}|c_{0},\bar{c}\rangle.

One finds that when relating

, we have used a strategy different from the case of

-symbols extracted at different positions. This is because defining

,

, and
M^{a}_{12}
(
