) sweep around
x_{1}
in a counter-clockwise manner. If one insist on having the other orientation, e.g. having all our set up mirror-imaged, the extracted matrices via Eq. (
155
) (denoted as
\bar{R}
) are related to
\tilde{R}
-symbols by

[(R^{ab}_{c})_{0121^{\prime}2^{\prime}34}]_{\mu\nu}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\})=\sum_{\nu^{\prime}}[(\tilde{R}^{ba}_{c})_{3124}]^{-1}_{\mu\nu^{\prime}}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\})[(\Gamma^{ab}_{c})_{212^{\prime}1^{\prime}}]_{\nu^{\prime}\nu}\frac{\gamma^{a}_{312}}{\gamma^{b}_{4122^{\prime}}\gamma^{c}_{1^{\prime}10}\gamma^{a}_{311^{\prime}}}.

(159)

The microscopic
R
-matrices also yield two consistency equations with microscopic
F
-matrices, analogous to the hexagon equations Eq. (
139
). Again, we temporarily suppress the notation and make implicit the dependence on the anyon states and string operators for notational simplicity. In the following equations, all three microscopic
(R^{ab}_{c})_{0_{i}12{1^{\prime}}_{i}{2^{\prime}}_{i}34}
-matrices share the same points
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
,
x_{3}
, and
x_{4}
, but the splitting processes of
F
and
R
can happen at various positions. There is also a point
x_{\tilde{3}}
in the expression, on which the explicit expression of the equation does not depend. (This can be seen from changing the position of
x_{\tilde{3}}
and verify that the explicit form does not change.) We make explicit the position dependence of all
R
-matrices, and the abbreviated notation
\{x_{i}\}
again represents the position dependence of
F
symbols
\{0_{i}1_{i}2_{i}3_{i}4_{i}{1^{\prime}}_{i}{2^{\prime}}_{i}{3^{\prime}}_{i}{4^{\prime}}_{i}5_{i}6_{i}7_{i}\}
. Be aware that
x_{k_{i}}
’s are different points from
x_{k}
for
k=1,2,3,4
. The first equation is

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\zeta^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}\sum_{\lambda\gamma}[(R^{ac}_{e})_{0121_{1}^{\prime}2_{1}^{\prime}34}]_{\alpha\lambda^{\prime}}[(\Gamma^{ac}_{e})_{212_{1}^{\prime}1_{1}^{\prime}}]_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}^{-1}\frac{\gamma^{c}_{4122_{1}^{\prime}}\gamma^{e}_{1_{1}^{\prime}10}\gamma^{a}_{311_{1}^{\prime}}}{\gamma^{a}_{312}}\times

(160)

\displaystyle[(\Gamma^{ac}_{e})_{214_{2}2_{2}}]_{\lambda\zeta^{\prime}}^{-1}[(\Gamma^{eb}_{d})_{213_{2}1_{2}}]_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}^{-1}[(F^{acb}_{d})_{\{x_{2}\}}]_{\{e,\zeta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\},\{g,\xi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}\}}[(\Gamma_{g}^{cb})_{214_{2}^{\prime}3_{2}^{\prime}}]_{\xi^{\prime}\gamma}[(\Gamma_{d}^{ag})_{212_{2}^{\prime}1_{2}^{\prime}}]_{\nu^{\prime}\nu}\times

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{7_{2}\tilde{3}24_{2}^{\prime}}^{b}\gamma_{6_{2}13_{2}^{\prime}}^{c}\gamma_{3_{2}^{\prime}122_{2}^{\prime}}^{g}\gamma_{5_{2}311_{2}^{\prime}}^{a}\gamma_{1_{2}^{\prime}10_{2}}^{d}}{\gamma^{c}_{6_{2}124_{2}}\gamma^{a}_{5_{2}312_{2}}\gamma^{e}_{2_{2}11_{2}}\gamma^{d}_{7_{2}\tilde{3}23_{2}}\gamma^{d}_{1_{2}10_{2}}}\times\frac{\gamma_{24_{2}2}^{c}\gamma_{12_{2}1}^{a}\gamma_{12_{2}1}^{e}\gamma_{11_{2}1}^{e}\gamma_{23_{2}2}^{b}\gamma_{11_{2}1}^{d}}{\gamma_{24_{2}^{\prime}2}^{b}\gamma_{13_{2}^{\prime}1}^{c}\gamma_{13_{2}^{\prime}1}^{g}\gamma_{22_{2}^{\prime}2}^{g}\gamma_{11_{2}^{\prime}1}^{a}\gamma_{11_{2}^{\prime}1}^{d}}\times

\displaystyle[(R^{bc}_{g})_{0121_{3}^{\prime}2_{3}^{\prime}34}]_{\kappa\nu^{\prime}}[(\Gamma^{ac}_{e})_{212_{3}^{\prime}1_{3}^{\prime}}]_{\nu^{\prime}\nu}^{-1}\frac{\gamma^{c}_{4122_{3}^{\prime}}\gamma^{g}_{1_{3}^{\prime}10}\gamma^{b}_{311_{3}^{\prime}}}{\gamma^{b}_{312}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}\iota^{\prime}\psi^{\prime}}\sum_{f\sigma\epsilon\phi}[(\Gamma^{ca}_{e})_{434_{4}2_{4}}]_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}^{-1}[(\Gamma^{eb}_{d})_{433_{4}1_{4}}]_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}^{-1}[(F^{cab}_{d})_{\{x_{4}\}}]_{\{e,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\},\{f,\epsilon^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}\}}[(\Gamma_{f}^{ab})_{214_{4}^{\prime}3_{4}^{\prime}}]_{\epsilon^{\prime}\epsilon}[(\Gamma_{d}^{cf})_{212_{4}^{\prime}1_{4}^{\prime}}]_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}\times

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{7_{4}\tilde{3}24_{4}^{\prime}}^{b}\gamma_{6_{4}13_{4}^{\prime}}^{a}\gamma_{3_{4}^{\prime}122_{4}^{\prime}}^{f}\gamma_{5_{4}311_{4}^{\prime}}^{c}\gamma_{1_{4}^{\prime}10_{4}}^{d}}{\gamma^{a}_{6_{4}124_{4}}\gamma^{c}_{5_{4}312_{4}}\gamma^{e}_{2_{4}11_{4}}\gamma^{d}_{7_{4}\tilde{3}23_{4}}\gamma^{d}_{1_{4}10_{4}}}\times\frac{\gamma_{24_{4}2}^{a}\gamma_{12_{4}1}^{c}\gamma_{12_{4}1}^{e}\gamma_{11_{4}1}^{e}\gamma_{23_{4}2}^{b}\gamma_{11_{4}1}^{d}}{\gamma_{24_{4}^{\prime}2}^{b}\gamma_{13_{4}^{\prime}1}^{a}\gamma_{13_{4}^{\prime}1}^{f}\gamma_{22_{4}^{\prime}2}^{f}\gamma_{11_{4}^{\prime}1}^{c}\gamma_{11_{4}^{\prime}1}^{d}}\times

\displaystyle[(R^{fc}_{d})_{\{0121_{5}^{\prime}2_{5}^{\prime}34\}}]_{\iota\psi^{\prime}}[(\Gamma^{fc}_{d})_{212_{5}^{\prime}1_{5}^{\prime}}]_{\psi^{\prime}\psi}^{-1}\frac{\gamma^{c}_{4122_{5}^{\prime}}\gamma^{d}_{1_{5}^{\prime}10}\gamma^{f}_{311_{5}^{\prime}}}{\gamma^{f}_{312}}\times

\displaystyle[(\Gamma^{ab}_{f})_{434_{6}2_{6}}]_{\epsilon\phi^{\prime}}^{-1}[(\Gamma^{fc}_{d})_{433_{6}1_{6}}]_{\psi\varphi^{\prime}}^{-1}[(F^{abc}_{d})_{\{x_{6}\}}]_{\{f,\phi^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}\},\{g,\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}\}}[(\Gamma_{g}^{bc})_{214_{6}^{\prime}3_{6}^{\prime}}]_{\mu^{\prime}\mu}[(\Gamma_{d}^{ag})_{212_{6}^{\prime}1_{6}^{\prime}}]_{\nu^{\prime}\nu}\times

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{7_{6}\tilde{3}24_{6}^{\prime}}^{c}\gamma_{6_{6}13_{6}^{\prime}}^{b}\gamma_{3_{6}^{\prime}122_{6}^{\prime}}^{g}\gamma_{5_{6}311_{6}^{\prime}}^{a}\gamma_{1_{6}^{\prime}10_{6}}^{d}}{\gamma^{b}_{6_{6}124_{6}}\gamma^{a}_{5_{6}312_{6}}\gamma^{f}_{2_{6}11_{6}}\gamma^{d}_{7_{6}\tilde{3}23_{6}}\gamma^{d}_{1_{6}10_{6}}}\times\frac{\gamma_{24_{6}2}^{b}\gamma_{12_{6}1}^{a}\gamma_{12_{6}1}^{f}\gamma_{11_{6}1}^{f}\gamma_{23_{6}2}^{c}\gamma_{11_{6}1}^{d}}{\gamma_{24_{6}^{\prime}2}^{c}\gamma_{13_{6}^{\prime}1}^{b}\gamma_{13_{6}^{\prime}1}^{g}\gamma_{22_{6}^{\prime}2}^{g}\gamma_{11_{6}^{\prime}1}^{a}\gamma_{11_{6}^{\prime}1}^{d}}.

The second equation is

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\zeta^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}\sum_{\lambda\gamma}[(R^{ca}_{e})_{0121_{1}^{\prime}2_{1}^{\prime}34}]^{-1}_{\alpha\lambda^{\prime}}[(\Gamma^{ca}_{e})_{212_{1}^{\prime}1_{1}^{\prime}}]_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}\frac{\gamma^{a}_{312}}{\gamma^{c}_{4122_{1}^{\prime}}\gamma^{e}_{1_{1}^{\prime}10}\gamma^{a}_{311_{1}^{\prime}}}\times

(161)

\displaystyle[(\Gamma^{ac}_{e})_{214_{2}2_{2}}]_{\lambda\zeta^{\prime}}^{-1}[(\Gamma^{eb}_{d})_{213_{2}1_{2}}]_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}^{-1}[(F^{acb}_{d})_{\{x_{2}\}}]_{\{e,\zeta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\},\{g,\xi^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}\}}[(\Gamma_{g}^{cb})_{214_{2}^{\prime}3_{2}^{\prime}}]_{\xi^{\prime}\gamma}[(\Gamma_{d}^{ag})_{212_{2}^{\prime}1_{2}^{\prime}}]_{\nu^{\prime}\nu}\times

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{7_{2}\tilde{3}24_{2}^{\prime}}^{b}\gamma_{6_{2}13_{2}^{\prime}}^{c}\gamma_{3_{2}^{\prime}122_{2}^{\prime}}^{g}\gamma_{5_{2}311_{2}^{\prime}}^{a}\gamma_{1_{2}^{\prime}10_{2}}^{d}}{\gamma^{c}_{6_{2}124_{2}}\gamma^{a}_{5_{2}312_{2}}\gamma^{e}_{2_{2}11_{2}}\gamma^{d}_{7_{2}\tilde{3}23_{2}}\gamma^{d}_{1_{2}10_{2}}}\times\frac{\gamma_{24_{2}2}^{c}\gamma_{12_{2}1}^{a}\gamma_{12_{2}1}^{e}\gamma_{11_{2}1}^{e}\gamma_{23_{2}2}^{b}\gamma_{11_{2}1}^{d}}{\gamma_{24_{2}^{\prime}2}^{b}\gamma_{13_{2}^{\prime}1}^{c}\gamma_{13_{2}^{\prime}1}^{g}\gamma_{22_{2}^{\prime}2}^{g}\gamma_{11_{2}^{\prime}1}^{a}\gamma_{11_{2}^{\prime}1}^{d}}\times

\displaystyle[(R^{cb}_{g})_{0121_{3}^{\prime}2_{3}^{\prime}34}]^{-1}_{\kappa\nu^{\prime}}[(\Gamma^{cb}_{e})_{212_{3}^{\prime}1_{3}^{\prime}}]_{\nu^{\prime}\nu}\frac{\gamma^{b}_{312}}{\gamma^{c}_{4122_{3}^{\prime}}\gamma^{g}_{1_{3}^{\prime}10}\gamma^{b}_{311_{3}^{\prime}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}\iota^{\prime}\psi^{\prime}}\sum_{f\sigma\epsilon\phi}[(\Gamma^{ca}_{e})_{434_{4}2_{4}}]_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}^{-1}[(\Gamma^{eb}_{d})_{433_{4}1_{4}}]_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}^{-1}[(F^{cab}_{d})_{\{x_{4}\}}]_{\{e,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\},\{f,\epsilon^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}\}}[(\Gamma_{f}^{ab})_{214_{4}^{\prime}3_{4}^{\prime}}]_{\epsilon^{\prime}\epsilon}[(\Gamma_{d}^{cf})_{212_{4}^{\prime}1_{4}^{\prime}}]_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}\times

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{7_{4}\tilde{3}24_{4}^{\prime}}^{b}\gamma_{6_{4}13_{4}^{\prime}}^{a}\gamma_{3_{4}^{\prime}122_{4}^{\prime}}^{f}\gamma_{5_{4}311_{4}^{\prime}}^{c}\gamma_{1_{4}^{\prime}10_{4}}^{d}}{\gamma^{a}_{6_{4}124_{4}}\gamma^{c}_{5_{4}312_{4}}\gamma^{e}_{2_{4}11_{4}}\gamma^{d}_{7_{4}\tilde{3}23_{4}}\gamma^{d}_{1_{4}10_{4}}}\times\frac{\gamma_{24_{4}2}^{a}\gamma_{12_{4}1}^{c}\gamma_{12_{4}1}^{e}\gamma_{11_{4}1}^{e}\gamma_{23_{4}2}^{b}\gamma_{11_{4}1}^{d}}{\gamma_{24_{4}^{\prime}2}^{b}\gamma_{13_{4}^{\prime}1}^{a}\gamma_{13_{4}^{\prime}1}^{f}\gamma_{22_{4}^{\prime}2}^{f}\gamma_{11_{4}^{\prime}1}^{c}\gamma_{11_{4}^{\prime}1}^{d}}\times

\displaystyle[(R^{cf}_{d})_{\{0121_{5}^{\prime}2_{5}^{\prime}34\}}]^{-1}_{\iota\psi^{\prime}}[(\Gamma^{cf}_{d})_{212_{5}^{\prime}1_{5}^{\prime}}]_{\psi^{\prime}\psi}^{-1}\frac{\gamma^{f}_{312}}{\gamma^{c}_{4122_{5}^{\prime}}\gamma^{d}_{1_{5}^{\prime}10}\gamma^{f}_{311_{5}^{\prime}}}\times

\displaystyle[(\Gamma^{ab}_{f})_{434_{6}2_{6}}]_{\epsilon\phi^{\prime}}^{-1}[(\Gamma^{fc}_{d})_{433_{6}1_{6}}]_{\psi\varphi^{\prime}}^{-1}[(F^{abc}_{d})_{\{x_{6}\}}]_{\{f,\phi^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}\},\{g,\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}\}}[(\Gamma_{g}^{bc})_{214_{6}^{\prime}3_{6}^{\prime}}]_{\mu^{\prime}\mu}[(\Gamma_{d}^{ag})_{212_{6}^{\prime}1_{6}^{\prime}}]_{\nu^{\prime}\nu}\times

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{7_{6}\tilde{3}24_{6}^{\prime}}^{c}\gamma_{6_{6}13_{6}^{\prime}}^{b}\gamma_{3_{6}^{\prime}122_{6}^{\prime}}^{g}\gamma_{5_{6}311_{6}^{\prime}}^{a}\gamma_{1_{6}^{\prime}10_{6}}^{d}}{\gamma^{b}_{6_{6}124_{6}}\gamma^{a}_{5_{6}312_{6}}\gamma^{f}_{2_{6}11_{6}}\gamma^{d}_{7_{6}\tilde{3}23_{6}}\gamma^{d}_{1_{6}10_{6}}}\times\frac{\gamma_{24_{6}2}^{b}\gamma_{12_{6}1}^{a}\gamma_{12_{6}1}^{f}\gamma_{11_{6}1}^{f}\gamma_{23_{6}2}^{c}\gamma_{11_{6}1}^{d}}{\gamma_{24_{6}^{\prime}2}^{c}\gamma_{13_{6}^{\prime}1}^{b}\gamma_{13_{6}^{\prime}1}^{g}\gamma_{22_{6}^{\prime}2}^{g}\gamma_{11_{6}^{\prime}1}^{a}\gamma_{11_{6}^{\prime}1}^{d}}.

When the
F
- and
R
-matrices coincide with categorical
F
- and
R
-symbols, which is the case when Eqs. (
11
), (
12
) and (
13
) hold, it is straightforward to check that the above equations reduce to the hexagon equations Eq. (
139
).

G.2.2
The microscopic transformation of
R
-matrices

Now we discuss how
R
-matrices vary when changing the three conditions we discussed in the previous section.

•

Changing the moving and splitting operators used to define
R
-matrices.

Under the transformation shown in Eq. (
21
), the two states
|\mu\rangle
and
|\nu\rangle
transform as

\displaystyle|\mu\rangle\to\sum_{\mu^{\prime}}e^{i\phi_{32}(a)}e^{i\phi_{41}(b)}[\Omega_{0}(b,a;c)_{21}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}e^{i\phi_{10}(c)}|\mu^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle|\nu\rangle\to\sum_{\nu^{\prime}}e^{i\phi_{42^{\prime}}(b)}e^{i\phi_{31^{\prime}}(a)}[\Omega_{0}(a,b;c)_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}}]_{\nu\nu^{\prime}}e^{i\phi_{1^{\prime}0}(c)}|\nu^{\prime}\rangle.

As such, the
R
-matrices transform as

\displaystyle{}_{\mu\nu}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\})\to

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}[\Omega_{0}(b,a;c)_{21}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}([R^{ab}_{c}]_{\mu\nu})_{\{x\}}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\}^{\prime})[\Omega_{0}(a,b;c)_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}}]_{\nu^{\prime}\nu}^{-1}

(162)

\displaystyle\times\frac{e^{i\phi_{32}(a)}e^{i\phi_{41}(b)}e^{i\phi_{10}(c)}}{e^{i\phi_{42^{\prime}}(b)}e^{i\phi_{31^{\prime}}(a)}e^{i\phi_{1^{\prime}0}(c)}}.

When the
R
-matrices coincide with the categorical
\tilde{R}
symbols, the above transformation is simplified to

[(R^{ab}_{c})_{\{x\}}]_{\mu\nu}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\})\to\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}\left(e^{i\phi_{12}(a)}[\Omega_{0}(b,a;c)_{21}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\right)[(R^{ab}_{c})_{\{x\}}]_{\mu\nu}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\})\left(e^{i\phi_{12}(b)}[\Omega_{0}(a,b;c)_{21}]_{\nu\nu^{\prime}}\right)^{-1}.

This simplified transformation is also introduced in Ref.
Kawagoe and Levin 2020
, which is precisely the same form as the vertex basis gauge transformation of
\tilde{R}
symbols in MTC.

•

Changing the local information of anyons.
When changing the local information of the chosen anyon states, one can use the same argument as in the case of
F
-matrices to show that the
R
-matrices can be invariant when we choose the moving and splitting operators in Eq. (
152
), and the
R
-matrices transform as Eq. (
162
) when we choose other moving and splitting operators.

•

Changing the position of anyons.

Since the definition of
R
-matrices has special requirements on moving operators, comparing two microscopic
R
-matrices defined at different positions is a bit more complicated than the case of
F
-matrices. Denote these two
R
-matrices as
(R^{ab}_{c})_{0_{1}1_{1}2_{1}1_{1}^{\prime}2_{1}^{\prime}3_{1}4_{1}}
and
(R^{ab}_{c})_{0_{2}1_{2}2_{2}1_{2}^{\prime}2_{2}^{\prime}3_{2}4_{2}}
. These
R
-matrices should be extracted with the setup of positions and moving operators introduced in Fig.
9
(repeated in Fig.
11
(a)), or be extracted with the mirror-imaged setup of the original setup, as shown in Fig.
11
(b). The strategy to establish their relations is the following. we pick
5
points
\tilde{x}_{0}\tilde{x}_{1}\tilde{x}_{2}\tilde{x}_{4}\tilde{x}_{5}
, with
M_{\tilde{1}\tilde{0}}
-
M_{\tilde{4}\tilde{0}}
-
M_{\tilde{2}\tilde{0}}
organized in a counter-clockwise direction and
M_{\tilde{1}\tilde{0}}
-
M_{\tilde{5}\tilde{0}}
-
M_{\tilde{2}\tilde{0}}
organized in a clockwise direction. As such, we can extract categorical
\tilde{R}
-symbols
(\tilde{R}^{ab}_{c})_{\tilde{x}_{0}\tilde{x}_{1}\tilde{x}_{2}\tilde{x}_{4}}
and
(\tilde{R}^{ab}_{c})_{\tilde{x}_{0}\tilde{x}_{1}\tilde{x}_{2}\tilde{x}_{5}}=(\tilde{R}^{ba}_{c})^{-1}_{\tilde{x}_{0}\tilde{x}_{1}\tilde{x}_{2}\tilde{x}_{4}}
(see Appendix
F

Again, the above relations are independent of the choice of the position
\tilde{0}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}\tilde{4}\tilde{5}
, the moving and splitting operators within
\tilde{0}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}\tilde{4}\tilde{5}
, the moving operators between
\tilde{0}\tilde{1}\tilde{2}\tilde{4}\tilde{5}
and
x_{0_{1}}x_{1_{1}}x_{2_{1}}x_{1_{1}^{\prime}}x_{2_{1}^{\prime}}x_{3_{1}}x_{4_{1}}

. This independence can be seen by picking other positions as
\{\tilde{x}\}
and re-defining the moving and splitting operators, which will result in precisely the same expression.

To summarize, when we change the moving and splitting operators, the
R
-matrices change according to Eq.(
162
); when we change anyon local information, the
R
-matrices can be invariant or transform according to Eq.(
162
) depending on the choices of the corresponding moving and splitting operators. When we change the anyon positions we used to define them, the
R
-matrices undergo a transformation given by Eqs. (
163
) and (
164
) depending on the orientation of the moving operators. When Eqs. (
11
), (
12
) and (
13
) hold, these transformation take the form of Eq. (
24
).

G.3
The change of vertex basis under symmetry transformation:
U
-matrices

As we have discussed in the main text, the symmetry properties of a TQSL includes the change of anyon types and fusion vertex basis states under symmetry transformations. These two properties are encoded in the
U
-matrices. The definition of the
U
-matrices is introduced in Eq. (
5
) of Sec.
III.2.3
, and also reformulated in Eq. (
26
) of Sec.
IV.3
. For the convenience of the reader, we repeat the definition in Eq. (
26
) here:

[U_{g}(a,b;c)_{12}]_{\mu\nu}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{S\},\{V\})=\langle\nu|\mu\rangle,

(165)

where

\displaystyle|\mu\rangle=R_{g}(S^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptb}_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptc,\mu})_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt2^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt1}R_{g}^{-1}V^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptc}_{g}(x_{1})|c_{0},\bar{c}\rangle,

(166)

\displaystyle|\nu\rangle=V^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{g}(x_{1})V^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptb}_{g}(x_{2})(S^{ab}_{c,\nu})_{21}|c_{0},\bar{c}\rangle.

In the main text, we focus on the case where Eqs. (
11
), (
12
) and (
13
) hold. However, in general, these conditions do not need to hold. In the following, we will discuss the properties of the
U
-matrices defined above, under the general conditions given by Eqs. (
140
), (
141
) and (
142
). Again, we focus on two questions: 1) When the choices of
\{|\psi\rangle\}
,
\{S\}
and
\{V\}
change, how do the
U
-matrices defined in Eq. (
165
) transform? And 2) What consistency equations should the
U
-matrices defined in Eq. (
165
) satisfy?

G.3.1
The microscopic transformation of
U
-matrices

We start with the transformation of the
U
-matrices defined in Eq. (
165
) when
\{|\psi\rangle\}
,
\{S\}
and
\{V\}
change.

•

Changing the moving and splitting operators used to define
U
-matrices.

Under the transformation shown in Eq. (
21
), the two states
|\mu\rangle
and
|\nu\rangle
transform as

\displaystyle|\mu\rangle\to\sum_{\mu^{\prime}}K^{q(g)}[\Omega_{0}(^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta,^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptb;^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptc)_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt2^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt1}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}K^{q(g)}|\mu^{\prime}\rangle,

\displaystyle|\nu\rangle\to\sum_{\nu^{\prime}}[\Omega_{0}(a,b;c)_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}}]_{\nu\nu^{\prime}}|\nu^{\prime}\rangle.

As such, the
U
-matrices transform as

\displaystyle[U_{g}(a,b;c)_{12}]_{\mu\nu}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\},\{V\})\to

(167)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}K^{q(g)}[\Omega_{0}(^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta,^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptb;^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptc)_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt2^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt1}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}K^{q(g)}[U_{g}(a,b;c)_{12}]_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{S\},\{V\})[\Omega_{0}(a,b;c)_{21}]^{-1}_{\nu^{\prime}\nu}.

When the symmetry is internal, the
U
-matrices are simplified to the
U
-symbols in a
G
-crossed braided tensor category. Moreover,
{}^{\bar{g}}x_{1,2}=x_{1,2}
, and the above transformation is simplified to

\displaystyle[U_{g}(a,b;c)_{12}]_{\mu\nu}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\},\{V\})\to

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}[\Omega_{0}(^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta,^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptb;^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptc)_{21}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}[U_{g}(a,b;c)_{12}]_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\},\{V\})[\Omega_{0}(a,b;c)_{21}]^{-1}_{\nu^{\prime}\nu},

which is precisely the vertex basis gauge transformation of the
U
-symbols in a
G
-crossed braided tensor category for TQSLs with an internal symmetry
G
.

•

Changing the
V
operators used to define
U
-matrices.

In defining the
U
-matrices, we need to insert
V
operators to compensate the change of anyon local information. These
V
operators, determined according to Eq. (
5
), are not unique either. In particular, according to the discussion around Eq. (
35
), re-defining these
V
operator yields

V^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{g}(x_{1})|a,b,c;\mu\rangle\to(\delta^{a}_{g}(x_{1}))^{-1}V^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{g}(x_{1})|a,b,c;\mu\rangle.

(168)

Under the re-definition in Eq. (
168
), two states
|\mu\rangle
and
|\nu\rangle
transform as

|\mu\rangle\to(\delta^{c}_{g}(x_{1}))^{-1}|\mu\rangle;\qquad|\nu\rangle\to(\delta^{a}_{g}(x_{1}))^{-1}(\delta^{b}_{g}(x_{2}))^{-1}|\nu\rangle.

As such, the
U
-matrices transform as

[U_{g}(a,b;c)_{12}]_{\mu\nu}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{S\},\{V\})\to[U_{g}(a,b;c)_{12}]_{\mu\nu})(|\psi\rangle,\{S\},\{V\})\frac{\delta^{a}_{g}(x_{1})\delta^{b}_{g}(x_{2})}{\delta^{c}_{g}(x_{1})}.

(169)

At the same time, under the re-definition in Eq. (
168
), the
\omega
phases in Eq. (
142
) also change correspondingly,

(\omega^{a}_{g})_{12}\to(\omega^{a}_{g})_{12}\delta^{a}_{g}(x_{1})(\delta^{a}_{g}(x_{2}))^{-1}.

So when Eq. (
13
) holds,
\delta^{a}_{g}(x_{1})(\delta^{a}_{g}(x_{2}))^{-1}=1
for all anyons
a
and all positions
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. Then the above transformation is simplified to

[U_{g}(a,b;c)_{12}]_{\mu\nu}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{S\},\{V\})\to\frac{\delta^{a}_{g}\delta^{b}_{g}}{\delta^{c}_{g}}[U_{g}(a,b;c)_{12}]_{\mu\nu}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{S\},\{V\}),

(170)

which is precisely the symmetry action gauge transformation of
U
-symbols in a
G
-crossed braided tensor category, no matter whether the TQSL under discussion has lattice symmetry or not. We have addressed this transformation in Sec.
IV.3.2
.

•

Changing the local information of the chosen anyon states.

When the local information around the anyons is changed, similar to the previous discussions on the
F
- and
R
-matrices, the transformation of the
U
-matrices is already captured by their transformations induced by changing the moving, splitting and
V
operators. Namely, the
U
-matrices transform according to Eqs. (
167
) and (
169
).

•

\displaystyle[U_{g}(a,b;c)_{12}]_{\mu\nu}=\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}K^{q(g)}\left[(\Gamma_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptc}^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptb})_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt4^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt3^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt2^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt1}\right]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}K^{q(g)}\times[U_{g}(a,b;c)_{34}]_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}\times

(171)

\displaystyle\left[(\Gamma_{c}^{ab})_{4321}\right]^{-1}_{\nu^{\prime}\nu}\times(\omega_{g}^{c})_{13}(\omega_{g}^{a})_{31}(\omega^{b}_{g})_{24}.

When the symmetry action
g
is internal (indicating that
{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptx_{i}=x_{i}
) and all the
\omega
phases are
1
, this transformation is simplified to the vertex basis gauge transformation of the
U
-symbols in a
G
-crossed braided tensor category for TQSLs with an internal symmetry
G
.

G.3.2
The consistency equations of microscopic
U
-matrices

The
U
-matrices need to obey some consistency equations that involve the
F
- and
R
-matrices. In particular, the consistency equation between the
U
- and
F
-matrices comes from the “commutativity” between the symmetry transformation and the fusion, and the equation between the
U
- and
R
-matrices comes from the “commutativity” between the symmetry transformation and the braiding of anyons.

). We introduce a notation

R^{ab}_{c}
; when

\bar{R}^{ab}_{c}
. In particular, for categorical
\tilde{R}
symbols,

for orientation preserving
g
and

for orientation reversing

. Summarizing the above process, the consistency equation between

- and

When

- and

-matrices are simplified to the categorical
F
, and all

-phases are

, the above two equations simplify to the consistency equations we introduced in the main text.

G.4
Symmetry fractionalization:
\eta
-phases

The last piece of universal data is the symmetry fractionalization of anyons. Physically, non-trivial symmetry fractionalization of an anyon means that an anyon fails to feel the global symmetry acting linearly. This deviation from linearity is characterized by a phase factor
\eta
, which is precisely the
\eta
phase we defined in the main text. We repeat the definition here.

\eta_{a}(g,h)_{1}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{V\})=\langle 1|2\rangle,

where

\displaystyle|1\rangle=V_{gh}^{{}^{\overline{gh}}a}(x_{1})|a_{1},\bar{a}\rangle,

\displaystyle|2\rangle=R_{g}V_{h}^{{}^{\overline{gh}}a}(^{\bar{g}}x_{1})R_{g}^{-1}V_{g}^{{}^{\bar{g}}a}(x_{1})|a_{1},\bar{a}\rangle.

The subscript
1
in
\eta_{a}(g,h)_{1}
is the position label, as before. We have proved in the main text that the two states
|1\rangle
and
|2\rangle
differ by merely a phase factor. Below we discuss the properties of the
\eta
-phases.

G.4.1
The microscopic transformation of the
\eta
-phases

Here, we discuss how
\eta
-phases change when the setup and relevant operators change. Since the definition of
\eta
-phases does not involve moving or splitting operators, we only need to discuss how
\eta
-phases when changing the local information of anyon states, changing the
V
operators, and changing the position.

•

Changing the
V
operators.

When the
V
operators transform as in Eq. (
168
), the two states transform as

\displaystyle|1\rangle\to(\delta^{a}_{gh}(x_{1}))^{-1}|1\rangle,

\displaystyle|2\rangle\to\left(K^{q(g)}\delta^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{h}(^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptx_{1})K^{q(g)}\delta^{a}_{g}(x_{1})\right)^{-1}|2\rangle.

Consequently, the
\eta
-phases change as

\eta_{a}(g,h))_{1}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{V\})\to\frac{\delta^{a}_{gh}(x_{1})}{K^{q(g)}\delta^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{h}(^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptx_{1})K^{q(g)}\delta^{a}_{g}(x_{1})}\eta_{a}(g,h)_{1}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{V\}).

(174)

When the symmetry is internal, i.e.,
{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptx=x
, the above transformation is simplified to the symmetry action gauge transformation of
\eta
-symbols in the
G
-crossed braided tensor category.

•

Changing the local information of anyon states.

When the local information around the anyons is changed, similar to the previous discussions on the
U
-matrices, the transformation of the
\eta
-phases is already captured by their transformations induced by changing the
V
operators. Namely, the
\eta
-phases transform according to Eq. (
174
).

•

Changing the position of defining the
\eta
-phases.

We find two
\eta_{a}(g,h)
symbols defined at two different positions
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. To find the relation between them, we apply the following process.

and

, the above transformation is simplified to

, which means
\eta
symbols are equal everywhere.

G.4.2
The consistency equation of
\eta
-phases

The
\eta
-phases also yield two consistency equations. The first consistency equation comes from the group multiplication of symmetry actions. In particular, successively applying
R_{g}
and
R_{h}
must be equivalent to applying
R_{gh}
(up to an irrelevant global phase factor, which is assumed to be
1
here). This consistency relation is derived as follows. Again we have made implicit the fact that the symmetry may transform the anyon positions. Keep noted that the operators in the shaded region act on the transformed positions.

. If we first extract the
\eta_{c}
-phase, it then follows to extract a
U_{gh}
-matrix defined at
x_{1}x_{2}
; if we first extract the
U_{h}
-matrix, we can then extract a
U_{g}
-matrix defined at
x_{1}x_{2}
and two
\eta
-phases defined at
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
respectively. Summarizing the above processes, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu}K^{q}(g)[U_{h}\left({}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta,^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptb;^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptc\right)_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt1^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt2}]_{\mu\nu}K^{q}(g)[U_{g}(a,b;c)_{12}]_{\nu\rho}=\frac{\eta_{c}(g,h)_{1}}{\eta_{a}(g,h)_{1}\eta_{b}(g,h)_{2}}[U_{gh}(a,b;c)_{12}]_{\mu\rho}.

(176)

Equation (
176
) is the consistency equation between
U
-matrices and
\eta
-phases. When all phases
\gamma=\omega=1
(no matter what
\Gamma
is), the consistency equation is simplified to Eq. (
32
). Apart from this equation, there is one more equation that
\eta
-phases themselves should satisfy. This second equation comes from the ‘associativity’ of symmetry actions onto a single anyon.

R_{h}^{-1}
R_{h}
R_{g}
R_{g}^{-1}
x_{1}
{}^{\overline{g}}x_{1}
{}^{\overline{gh}}x_{1}
R_{h}^{-1}
R_{h}
R_{g}
R_{g}^{-1}
x_{1}
{}^{\overline{gh}}x_{1}
R_{g}
R_{g}^{-1}
x_{1}
{}^{\overline{g}}x_{1}
x_{1}
K^{q(g)}\eta_{{}^{\overline{g}}a}(h,k)_{{}^{\overline{g}}1}K^{q(g)}
V_{g}^{{}^{\overline{g}}a}(x_{1})
V_{h}^{{}^{\overline{gh}}a}(^{\overline{g}}x_{1})
V_{k}^{{}^{\overline{ghk}}a}(^{\overline{gh}}x_{1})
V_{g}^{{}^{\overline{g}}a}(x_{1})
V_{hk}^{{}^{\overline{ghk}}a}(^{\overline{g}}x_{1})
\eta_{a}(g,h)_{1}
\eta_{a}(g,hk)_{1}
\eta_{a}(gh,k)_{1}
V_{gh}^{{}^{\overline{gh}}a}(x_{1})
V_{k}^{{}^{\overline{ghk}}a}(^{\overline{gh}}x_{1})
V_{ghk}^{{}^{\overline{ghk}}a}(x_{1})

The above figure shows two different ways that both start from the upper-left figure and end in the bottom-right figure, and the two ways must be equal. The equivalence results in the consistency of
\eta
-phases,

K^{q(g)}\eta_{{}^{\overline{g}}a}(h,k)_{{}^{\overline{g}}1}K^{q(g)}\eta_{a}(g,h)_{1}=\eta_{a}(g,hk)_{1}\eta_{a}(gh,k)_{1}.

(177)

When all phases
\gamma=\omega=1
(no matter what
\Gamma
is), this equation is precisely Eq. (
33
) in the main text.

G.5
The graded structure of the microscopic universal data

Having stated the general version of the universal data, we now discuss the structure of the general data. As we have seen, the general data is quite complicated and has explicit dependence on various subjective choices. In fact, this universal data admits a graded structure parameterized by
\gamma
,
\Gamma
, and
\omega
. Denote the full collection of the universal data as
\mathcal{C}
, which includes all possible data defined at various positions to put anyons, for different anyon states, and for different moving and splitting operators and
V
operators. Then

\mathcal{C}=\bigoplus_{\gamma,\Gamma,\omega}\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\Gamma,\omega},

(178)

where each
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\Gamma,\omega}
is the collection of data defined with fixed
\gamma
,
\Gamma
, and
\omega
that are introduced in Eqs. (
8
), (
9
) and (
10
).

Notice that for fixed
\gamma
,
\Gamma
, and
\omega
, we still have some residual freedom to change the moving and splitting operators and the
V
operators. In particular, if we change the operators as

\displaystyle M^{a}_{21}|a_{1},\bar{a}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle\to e^{i\phi^{a}_{21}}M^{a}_{21}|a_{1},\bar{a}_{0}\rangle,

(179)

\displaystyle(S^{ab}_{c;\mu})_{21}|c_{1},\bar{c}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle\to\sum_{\mu^{\prime}}[(\Omega_{0}(a,b;c)_{21}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}(S^{ab}_{c;\mu})_{21}|c_{1},\bar{c}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle,

\displaystyle V^{a}_{g}(x_{1})|^{g}\hskip-1.0pta_{1},^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{a}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle\to e^{i\varphi^{a}_{g}(x_{1})}V^{a}_{g}(x_{1})|^{g}\hskip-1.0pta_{1},^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{a}_{0^{\prime}}\rangle,

with the condition

\displaystyle e^{i\phi^{a}_{21}}e^{i\phi^{a}_{32}}=e^{i\phi^{a}_{31}},\quad e^{i\phi^{a}_{21}}=e^{-i\phi^{a}_{12}};

(180)

\displaystyle[\Omega_{0}(a,b;c)_{21}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}=[\Omega_{0}(a,b;c)_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\frac{e^{i\phi^{a}_{11^{\prime}}}e^{i\phi^{b}_{22^{\prime}}}}{e^{i\phi^{c}_{11^{\prime}}}};

\displaystyle e^{i\varphi^{a}_{g}(x_{1})}e^{i\phi^{a}_{12}}=K^{q(g)}e^{i\phi^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt1^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt2}}K^{q(g)}e^{i\varphi^{a}_{g}(x_{2})},

one can verify that
\gamma
,
\Gamma
, and
\omega
are invariant, while the universal data undergo a transformation defined in Eqs. (
151
), (
162
), (
167
), (
169
), and (
174
). We repeat the transformations here and use the condition Eq. (
180
) to simplify them. For
F
- and
R
-matrices, the transformations are

\displaystyle[(F^{abc}_{d})_{\{x\}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu\},\{f,\rho,\sigma\}}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\})\to\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}\left(e^{i\phi^{b}_{12}}[\Omega_{0}(a,b;e)_{21}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\right)\left(e^{i\phi^{c}_{12}}[\Omega_{0}(e,c;d)_{21}]_{\nu\nu^{\prime}}\right)

(181)

\displaystyle[(F^{abc}_{d})_{\{x\}}]_{\{e,\mu,\nu^{\prime}\},\{f,\rho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}\}}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\})\left(e^{i\phi^{c}_{12}}[\Omega_{0}(b,c;f)_{21}]_{\rho^{\prime}\rho}\right)^{-1}\left(e^{i\phi^{f}_{12}}[\Omega_{0}(a,f;d)_{21}]_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}\right)^{-1},

\displaystyle[(R^{ab}_{c})_{\{x\}}]_{\mu\nu}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\})\to\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}\left(e^{i\phi^{a}_{12}}[\Omega_{0}(b,a;c)_{21}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\right)[(R^{ab}_{c})_{\{x\}}]_{\mu\nu}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\})\left(e^{i\phi^{b}_{12}}[\Omega_{0}(a,b;c)_{21}]_{\nu^{\prime}\nu}\right)^{-1}.

These transformations are in the same form as the vertex basis gauge transformation of categorical
\tilde{F}
and
\tilde{R}
symbols, although the definition of
F
- and
R
- matrices are general. For
U
-matrices, the transformation is

\displaystyle[U_{g}(a,b;c)_{12}]_{\mu\nu}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\},\{V\})\to\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}K^{q(g)}\left(e^{i\phi^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptb}_{12}}[\Omega_{0}(^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta,^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptb;^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptc)_{21}]_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\right)K^{q(g)}\times

(182)

\displaystyle[U_{g}(a,b;c)_{12}]_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{M\},\{S\},\{V\})\left(e^{i\phi^{b}_{12}}[\Omega_{0}(a,b;c)_{21}]_{\nu^{\prime}\nu}\right)^{-1}\times K^{q(g)}\frac{e^{i\phi^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt11}}e^{i\phi^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptb}_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt11}}}{e^{i\phi^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptc}_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pt11}}}K^{q(g)}\frac{e^{i\varphi^{c}_{g}(x_{1})}}{e^{i\varphi^{a}_{g}(x_{1})}e^{i\varphi^{b}_{g}(x_{1})}},

which is a hybrid of the vertex basis gauge transformation and the symmetry action gauge transformation (as defined in Sec.
IV.3.2
). Finally, the
\eta
-phases transform as

\displaystyle\eta_{a}(g,h)_{1}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{V\})

\displaystyle\to\frac{K^{q(g)}e^{i\varphi_{h}^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}(^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptx_{1})}K^{q(g)}e^{i\varphi_{g}^{a}(x_{1})}}{e^{i\varphi_{gh}^{a}(x_{1})}}\eta_{a}(g,h)_{1}(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{V\})

(183)

\displaystyle=\frac{K^{q(g)}e^{i\varphi_{h}^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}(x_{1})}K^{q(g)}e^{i\varphi_{g}^{a}(x_{1})}}{e^{i\varphi_{gh}^{a}(x_{1})}}\frac{K^{q(gh)}e^{i\phi^{{}^{\overline{gh}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{{}^{\overline{gh}}\hskip-1.0pt11}}K^{q(gh)}}{K^{q(g)}e^{i\phi^{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{{}^{\overline{g}}\hskip-1.0pt11}}K^{q(h)}e^{i\phi^{{}^{\overline{gh}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{{}^{\overline{h}}\hskip-1.0pt11}}K^{q(gh)}}\eta_{a}(g,h)(\{|\psi\rangle\},\{V\}),

where we have used Eqs. (
180
) to eliminate
e^{i\varphi^{a}_{h}(^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0ptx_{1})}
.

The transformations Eq. (
181
)-(
183
) define the equivalence relation within a certain
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\Gamma,\omega}
, and the full universal data is therefore graded by
\gamma
,
\Gamma
, and
\omega
.

We note that the set of data
\mathcal{C}_{1,\mathbbm{1},1}
is not special from a microscopic perspective. However, it is the simplest among all
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\Gamma,\omega}
’s. In particular, the data in
\mathcal{C}_{1,\mathbbm{1},1}
is the closest to the topological quantum field theory. For example, the
F
- and
R
-matrices in
\mathcal{C}_{1,\mathbbm{1},1}
can be viewed as the
F
- and
R
-symbols in a unitary modular tensor category, as one can easily check, and their equivalence relations are precisely the vertex basis gauge transformations in such a category; the
U
-matrices and
\eta
-phases in
\mathcal{C}_{1,\mathbbm{1},1}
, when restricted to the internal symmetries, are precisely the
U
- and
\eta
-symbols in a
G
-crossed braided tensor category, and their equivalence relations match those in category theory correspondingly. Also, the condition
\gamma=1
and
\Gamma=\mathbbm{1}
correspond to the fact that the anyon worldlines and vertices can move freely in topological quantum field theory, and the condition
\omega=1
corresponds to that the group representation matrices can be smoothly translated via the Wilson lines. Therefore,
\mathcal{C}_{1,\mathbbm{1},1}
is special in the sense that it is potentially the data that has the nicest mathematical description. Another reason for
\mathcal{C}_{1,\mathbbm{1},1}
to be special is that the consistency equations of the data in
\mathcal{C}_{1,\mathbbm{1},1}
are position-independent. By inspecting these consistency equations, we are able to write down an exact crystalline equivalence principle.

Appendix H
The crystalline equivalence principle

In this appendix, we explain the details of our precise crystalline equivalence principle discussed in Sec.
V
, which is an explicit bijective map between the universal data characterizing SET phases with a general symmetry
G
and SET phases with a purely internal symmetry
G
, given by Eq. (
39
). According to Sec.
IV
and Appendix
G
, the universal properties of an SET phase can be characterized by
\mathcal{C}_{1,\mathbbm{1},1}
, which consists of the anyon types, fusion rules in Eq. (
1
),
F
-symbols in Eq. (
16
) and
R
-symbols in Eq. (
19
), the transformation of the anyon types
\rho_{g}(a)
in Eq. (
25
), the
U
-symbols in Eq. (
26
) and the
\eta
-symbols in Eq. (
29
). We will achieve the following goals in this appendix. First, in Appendix
H.1
we will give more details of the map in Eq. (
39
), including the meaning of
A
-matrices there and the inverse of the map Eq. (
39
). Second, in Appendix
H.2
we will apply the map Eq. (
39
) to the data
\mathcal{C}_{1,\mathbbm{1},1}
for an SET phase with a general symmetry
G
, which satisfies the consistency equations given by Eqs. (
203
), (
204
), (
205
) and (
206
), and show that the resulting data satisfies the consistency equations of the data
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1,\mathbbm{1},1}
for an SET phase with an internal symmetry
G
, given by Eqs. (
207
), (
208
), (
209
) and (
210
). Third, in Appendix
H.3
we will see that if a “gauge transformation” given by Eqs. (
24
) and (
34
) is applied to a set of data
\mathcal{C}_{1,\mathbbm{1},1}
for an SET phase with a general symmetry
G
, the mapped data
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1,\mathbbm{1},1}
for an SET phase with an internal symmetry
G
receives a “gauge transformation” given by Eqs. (
220
) and (
221
). Finally, in Appendix
H.4
we give an intuitive explanation for why the emergent CRT symmetry should be related to the crystalline equivalence principle, instead of the emergent RT symmetry.

Note that we have already shown that the data in
\mathcal{C}_{1,\mathbbm{1},1}
can be organized as some generalization of a category, so in this appendix we will employ many tools in category theory.

H.1
Details of the map Eq. (
39
)

We begin with explaining more details of the map in Eq. (
39
). For the readers’ convenience, Eq. (
39
) is copied here:

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}_{g}(a)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\rho_{g}(a)=^{g}a,&\textrm{if $g$ preserves spatial orientation,}\\
\rho_{g}(\bar{a})=^{g}\bar{a},&\textrm{if $g$ reverses spatial orientation.}\end{array}\right.

(184)

\displaystyle U^{\prime}_{g}(a,b;c)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}U_{g}(a,b;c),&\textrm{if $g$ preserves spatial orientation,}\\
K^{q(g)}(A^{{}^{\overline{g}}\hskip-1.0ptb^{\overline{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}_{{}^{\overline{g}}\hskip-1.0ptc}R^{{}^{\overline{g}}\hskip-1.0pta^{\overline{g}}\hskip-1.0ptb}_{{}^{\overline{g}}\hskip-1.0ptc})K^{q(g)}U_{g}(a,b;c),&\textrm{if $g$ reverses spatial orientation.}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}_{a}(g_{1},g_{2})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\eta_{a}(g_{1},g_{2}),&\textrm{if $g_{1}$ preserves spatial orientation,}\\
\eta_{a}(g_{1},g_{2})K^{q(g_{1})}U_{g_{2}}(^{\overline{g_{1}}}\hskip-1.0pta,^{\overline{g_{1}}}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{a};0)K^{q(g_{1})},&\textrm{if $g_{1}$ reverses spatial orientation but $g_{2}$ preserves it,}\\
\eta_{a}(g_{1},g_{2})K^{q(g_{1})}U_{g_{2}}(^{\overline{g_{1}}}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{a},^{\overline{g_{1}}}\hskip-1.0pta;0)\theta_{{}^{\overline{g_{1}}}\hskip-1.0pta}\varkappa^{*}_{{}^{\overline{g_{1}}}\hskip-1.0pta}K^{q(g_{1})},&\textrm{if both $g_{1}$ and $g_{2}$ reverse spatial orientation.}\end{array}\right.

Here the phase factor
\varkappa_{a}
is the Frobenius-Schur indicator
\varkappa_{a}=d_{a}[F^{a\bar{a}a}_{a}]_{00}
, where
d_{a}
is called the quantum dimension of the anyon
a
(see, for example, equation (8.10) in Ref.
Simon 2023
for its definition). Below we explain the meaning of the unitary matrix
A
in Appendix
H.1.1
, which is related to the emergent CRT symmetry of the underlying topological quantum field theory that describes the TQSL. Then we describe the inverse of the map Eq. (
39
) in Appendix
H.1.2
.

H.1.1
The CRT action

In the categorical description of anyons (see Refs.
Kitaev 2006
and
Simon 2023
for more introduction), the CRT symmetry naturally induces an
anti-unitary
map
\mathcal{A}
between the vertex spaces

\mathcal{A}:\quad V^{ab}_{c}\rightarrow V^{\bar{b}\bar{a}}_{\bar{c}}.

Here, the space
V^{ab}_{c}
is the vertex space of an anyon
c
splitting into
a
and
b
, which can be viewed as a vector space consisting of the morphisms whose source object is
c
and target object is
a\otimes b
, i.e.,
V^{ab}_{c}=
Hom
(c,a\otimes b)
; we will interchangeably call them “morphisms” or “vectors”. Also, we use the following graphic representation to denote the basis of
V^{ab}_{c}
,
12
12

12

From now on, we will use diagrammatic representations of the anyons to illustrate and determine the properties of the
A
map. The reader is recommended to refer to Ref.
Simon 2023
and appendix E of Ref.
Kitaev 2006
for details of the diagrammatic calculations. The primary rule of the calculation is that the anyon worldlines can freely move without bending them or changing their order.

As we will describe in Eq. (
185
), the unitary matrix
A
is the action of
\mathcal{A}
in a basis (note that
\mathcal{A}
can be viewed as a composition of a unitary map and a complex conjugation operation).

The map
\mathcal{A}
is constructed as follows. For a vector
|\mu\rangle=V^{ab}_{c}
, we first find its Hermitian conjugate

Then we choose three morphisms
|\eta_{a}\rangle\in V^{\bar{a}a}_{0}
,
|\eta_{b}\rangle\in V^{\bar{b}b}_{0}
, and
\langle\eta_{\bar{c}}|\in V^{0}_{c\bar{c}}
. These three morphisms can be arbitrary but are fixed for
\mathcal{A}
. Composing the above three morphisms with
\langle\mu^{\dagger}|
in the following way gives a morphism
\mathcal{A}(|\mu\rangle)\in V^{\bar{b}\bar{a}}_{\bar{c}}
,

(185)

This equation defines the matrix
[A^{\bar{b}\bar{a}}_{\bar{c}}]_{\mu\nu}
, which encodes the expansion coefficients of the output morphism
\mathcal{A}(|\mu\rangle)
in terms of the basis of
V^{\bar{b}\bar{a}}_{\bar{c}}
, which depends on the bases of
V^{ab}_{c}
,
V^{\bar{b}\bar{a}}_{\bar{c}}
,
V^{\bar{a}a}_{0}
,
V^{\bar{b}b}_{0}
, and
V^{0}_{c\bar{c}}
. The factor
\varkappa_{a}
is a phase defined as

(186)

The factor
\varkappa_{a}
can be derived from the
F
-moves
\varkappa_{a}=d_{a}F^{a\bar{a}a}_{d}
(see, for example, equation (14.2) in Ref.
Simon 2023
) and satisfies
\varkappa_{a}=\varkappa_{\bar{a}}^{*}
(see equation (194) in Ref.
Kitaev 2006
). For specific anyons
\bar{a}_{0}=a_{0}
,
\varkappa_{a_{0}}
is gauge invariant and coincides with the Frobenius-Schur indicator. We take out this factor in the definition Eq. (
185
) to ensure the
A
-matrices to have some nice normalization properties, as we will see below. We can see that the map
\mathcal{A}
is anti-unitary, because the input vector
|\mu\rangle
undergoes a Hermitian conjugate in the construction, and multiplying a constant to the vector
c_{1}|\mu_{1}\rangle
gives
c_{1}^{*}\mathcal{A}(|\mu_{1}\rangle)
.

In the rest of Appendix
H.1.1
, we discuss some properties of the matrices
A
that will be used later in Appendix
H
.

Theorem H.1
.

The unitary matrices
A^{ab}_{c}
satisfy the following equations.

\displaystyle(A^{\bar{b}\bar{a}}_{\bar{c}})^{*}A^{ab}_{c}=\frac{\varkappa_{a}\varkappa_{b}}{\varkappa_{c}},

(187)

\displaystyle A^{\bar{b}\bar{a}}_{\bar{e}}A^{\bar{c}\bar{e}}_{\bar{d}}(F^{\bar{c}\bar{b}\bar{a}}_{\bar{d}})_{\bar{e}\bar{f}}^{*}=(F^{abc}_{d})_{ef}^{*}A^{\bar{c}\bar{b}}_{\bar{f}}A^{\bar{f}\bar{a}}_{\bar{d}},

(188)

\displaystyle(R^{\bar{a}\bar{b}}_{\bar{c}})^{T}=A^{ba}_{c}R^{ab}_{c}(A^{ab}_{c})^{-1},

(189)

\displaystyle K^{q(g)}A^{\bar{b}\bar{a}}_{\bar{c}}K^{q(g)}U_{g}(^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{b},^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{a};^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{c})=\frac{U_{g}(^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{a},^{g}\hskip-1.0pta;0)U_{g}(^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{b},^{g}\hskip-1.0ptb;0)}{U_{g}(^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{c},^{g}\hskip-1.0ptc;0)}U_{g}^{*}(^{g}\hskip-1.0pta,^{g}\hskip-1.0ptb;^{g}\hskip-1.0ptc)A^{{}^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{b}^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{a}}_{{}^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{c}}.

(190)

Here the
F
- and
R
-matrices are the
F
- and
R
-symbols in the unitary modular tensor category, and the
U_{g}
-matrices represent the action of certain automorphism
\Phi_{g}
of the anyon types.
\Phi_{g}
is sometimes called the topological symmetry of the TQSL
Barkeshli et al. 2019a
, and it may microscopiclly originate from an internal or lattice symmetry, i.e.,
U_{g}
here can be viewed as
U_{g}
in the data
\mathcal{C}_{1,\mathbbm{1},1}
.

Proof.

1.

Proof of Eq. (
187
).

Applying two
\mathcal{A}
maps to a vector
|\mu\rangle
should yield the following condition.

\mathcal{A}

The figure on the bottom-left corner differs from the upper-left figure by merely bending the anyon lines, leading to a phase factor
\varkappa_{a}\varkappa_{b}\varkappa_{c}
(see, e.g., chapter 14 of Ref.
Simon 2023
). Then, using our definition of
A
-matrices Eq. (
185
), we find the resultant symbolic expression of this condition is Eq. (

).

The above two processes should be equal because we are merely performing basis transformations or re-phrasing the vector, and hence all the diagrams in the above represent the same vector. Again, in the above figure and in the following, we use the double-line arrow “
\Rightarrow
” to indicate that the diagram on the tail side of the arrow, excluding the factors, is equal to the diagram on the tip side multiplying the factors on the tip side or around the arrow. One multiplies together the factors appearing in the diagram and finds the correct relation or equation. Expressing the equation in symbolic form, we get Eq. (
188
).

4.

Proof of Eq. (
190
).

The
A
-symbols also yield certain consistency equations with the
U
-matrices. As we have mentioned, these
U
-matrices can be viewed as basis transformation matrices of certain automorphism
\Phi_{g}
, where
\Phi_{g}
might correspond to internal or lattice symmetry in the physical system. Then we notice that the definition of
A
-matrices yields

\overline{a}
\overline{b}
\overline{c}
{}^{g}\overline{a}
{}^{g}\overline{b}
{}^{g}\overline{c}
\overline{a}
\overline{b}
\overline{c}
{}^{g}\overline{a}
{}^{g}\overline{b}
{}^{g}\overline{c}
\mu^{\dagger}
\mu^{\prime}
{}^{g}\mu^{\prime}
{}^{g}\mu^{\dagger}
\Phi_{g}
\Phi_{g}
=\varkappa_{\overline{c}}A_{\overline{c}}^{\overline{b}\overline{a}}\times
=\varkappa_{{}^{g}\overline{c}}A_{{}^{g}\overline{c}}^{{}^{g}\overline{b}^{g}\overline{a}}\times

Note that the diagram in the upper-left corner consists of four vectors, and hence applying
\Phi_{g}
changes the basis of all four vectors. The symbolic expression of the diagram reads

K^{q(g)}\varkappa_{\bar{c}}A^{\bar{b}\bar{a}}_{\bar{c}}K^{q(g)}U_{g}(^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{b},^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{a};^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{c})=\frac{U_{g}(^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{a},^{g}\hskip-1.0pta;0)U_{g}(^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{b},^{g}\hskip-1.0ptb;0)}{U_{g}(^{g}\hskip-1.0ptc,^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{c};0)}U_{g}^{*}(^{g}\hskip-1.0pta,^{g}\hskip-1.0ptb;^{g}\hskip-1.0ptc)\varkappa_{{}^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{c}}A^{{}^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{b}^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{a}}_{{}^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{c}}.

We use the explicit expression of
\varkappa_{a}=d_{a}F^{a\bar{a}a}_{a}
to find its transformation under
\Phi_{g}
, which is

K^{q(g)}\varkappa_{{}^{\bar{g}}\hskip-1.0pta}K^{q(g)}=U_{g}(a,\bar{a};0)\varkappa_{a}U_{g}(\bar{a},a;0)^{-1}.

(191)

Substituting
\frac{\varkappa_{{}^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{c}}}{U_{g}(^{g}\hskip-1.0ptc,^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{c};0)}
with
\frac{K^{q(g)}\varkappa_{c}K^{q(g)}}{U_{g}(^{g}\hskip-1.0pt\bar{c},^{g}\hskip-1.0ptc;0)}
, we find the desired equation.

∎

It can be seen that the
A
-symbols essentially change anyons to their conjugates. Note that this conjugation cannot be viewed as a microscopic charge conjugation symmetry, because they neither obey the consistency equation for a charge conjugation symmetry (in particular, Eq. (
188
) is not the equation satisfied by the data characterizing an SET phase with an internal symmetry), nor do they have a guaranteed microscopic realization in a given TQSL. This
\mathcal{A}
map is interpreted as a CRT action at the level of the low-energy effective topological quantum field theory. We use this
\mathcal{A}
map, or the
A
-matrices, in Eq. (
39
) to construct the crystalline equivalence principle.

Theorem H.2
.

A^{\bar{a}a}_{0}R^{a\bar{a}}_{0}=A^{a\bar{a}}_{0}R^{\bar{a}a}_{0}=\theta^{*}_{a},

(192)

where
\theta_{a}
is the topological spin of the anyon
a
defined by

(193)

See, for example, Eq. (211) in Ref.
Kitaev 2006
for this definition.

Proof.

We consider the following equalities.

=A_{0}^{a\overline{a}}\left(R_{0}^{\overline{a}a}\right)^{T}
a
\overline{a}
\equiv
a
\overline{a}
=d_{a}\theta_{a}^{*}
=A_{0}^{a\overline{a}}\left(R_{0}^{\overline{a}a}\right)^{T}d_{a}

(194)

The equality between the first two figures on the upper line is due to our definition of
A
-matrices and the condition Eq. (
189
), and the second equality is the definition of the quantum dimension
d_{a}
. See, for example, Eq. (191) in Ref.
Kitaev 2006
for this definition. The two figures on the first row are equal because of the naturality of braiding, which allows us to freely move the cross, and the equality in the last line is the definition of topological spin Eq. (
193
). Combining all the equalities and using the fact that
R^{a\bar{a}}_{0}
are phases, we get Eq. (
192
).
∎

Apart from the equations above, there is also another useful equation we will use below:

R^{ab}_{e}R^{ec}_{d}(F^{abc}_{d})_{ef}=(F^{cba}_{d})_{ef}^{*}R^{bc}_{f}R^{af}_{d}.

(195)

This equation is derived from the following commutative diagram.

a
